    *   **Lack of Error Analysis:** No deep error analysis is presented for the evaluated models. Understanding the types of errors the LLMs are making could help improve their performance.

*   **Potential Influence:** The dataset has the potential to become a valuable resource for the misinformation research community, especially those focused on political communication and elections. The codebook could be adapted and extended for use in other countries and contexts. The baselines established in the paper can serve as a starting point for future model development.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:** The paper presents a novel and useful dataset for studying misleading narratives in the context of UK elections. The well-defined taxonomy and human annotation process are key strengths. However, the relatively small size of the dataset, the limited benchmarking experimentation and the UK-centric focus temper the significance of the contribution. This is more than a simple replication study because of the unique dataset but it's not groundbreaking.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Bring Reason to Vision: Understanding Perception and Reasoning through Model Merging
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05464v1)
- **Authors**: Shiqi Chen, Jinghan Zhang, Tongyao Zhu, Wei Liu, Siyang Gao, Miao Xiong, Manling Li, Junxian He
- **Abstract**: Vision-Language Models (VLMs) combine visual perception with the general capabilities, such as reasoning, of Large Language Models (LLMs). However, the mechanisms by which these two abilities can be combined and contribute remain poorly understood. In this work, we explore to compose perception and reasoning through model merging that connects parameters of different models. Unlike previous works that often focus on merging models of the same kind, we propose merging models across modalities, enabling the incorporation of the reasoning capabilities of LLMs into VLMs. Through extensive experiments, we demonstrate that model merging offers a successful pathway to transfer reasoning abilities from LLMs to VLMs in a training-free manner. Moreover, we utilize the merged models to understand the internal mechanism of perception and reasoning and how merging affects it. We find that perception capabilities are predominantly encoded in the early layers of the model, whereas reasoning is largely facilitated by the middle-to-late layers. After merging, we observe that all layers begin to contribute to reasoning, whereas the distribution of perception abilities across layers remains largely unchanged. These observations shed light on the potential of model merging as a tool for multimodal integration and interpretation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Bring Reason to Vision: Understanding Perception and Reasoning through Model Merging" explores a method for enhancing vision-language models (VLMs) by merging their parameters with those of large language models (LLMs) specifically trained for reasoning tasks, particularly math reasoning.  The core idea is to transfer reasoning capabilities from LLMs to VLMs without requiring further training. The authors demonstrate that this merging process consistently improves the performance of VLMs on math-related benchmarks.  Furthermore, the paper investigates how perception and reasoning are encoded within the VLM's parameter space, using knockout analysis and masking techniques. The key findings are that perception abilities are largely concentrated in the early layers of the model, while reasoning abilities are primarily facilitated by the middle-to-late layers. The merging process brings reasoning abilities to all layers while leaving the distribution of perception abilities largely unchanged.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of merging models isn't entirely new. Previous work has focused on merging models of the *same* kind (e.g., multiple LLMs).  The novelty here lies in merging *across modalities* (LLM with VLM) to transfer reasoning capabilities. The use of this merging as an interpretability tool is also somewhat novel, providing insights into the distribution of functions within VLM layers. However, the merging technique itself (linear merging) is well-established.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to address the limitations of current VLMs in complex multimodal reasoning. Transferring reasoning abilities from LLMs to VLMs without retraining could lead to more capable and efficient models.  The interpretability aspect is also significant because it helps us understand how these models combine visual perception and language reasoning, paving the way for more controlled model design and improvement.  The findings that perception and reasoning are somewhat disentangled in different layers of VLM architectures is valuable information.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Empirical Validation:**  The paper provides strong empirical evidence of improved performance on multiple math-related benchmarks through model merging. The inclusion of several VLMs and reasoning task vectors demonstrates the generalizability of the approach.

    *   **Interpretability Analysis:** The use of masking and knockout analysis to understand the distribution of perception and reasoning abilities is a valuable contribution.

    *   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents its methodology, results, and conclusions.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Merging Techniques:** The paper primarily focuses on linear merging. Exploring and comparing more sophisticated merging techniques (e.g., those based on task arithmetic or parameter importance) could strengthen the work.  The Appendix mentions TIES, but further investigation is warranted.

    *   **Dataset Specificity:** The focus on math-related tasks, while justifiable, limits the generalizability of the findings. It would be beneficial to explore the transfer of other types of reasoning skills (e.g., common-sense reasoning, causal reasoning).

    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** The performance of model merging can be sensitive to the choice of merging weights. While the authors perform hyperparameter tuning, the search space and tuning methodology could be described in more detail.  A more robust analysis of the sensitivity to these parameters would improve the reliability of the conclusions.

    *   **Limited Investigation of Negative Transfer:** The paper briefly acknowledges that merging can sometimes lead to a slight decrease in performance on general VQA tasks. A more thorough investigation of these negative transfer effects would be valuable. What characteristics of tasks or models make them susceptible to negative transfer?
*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper could influence future research in multimodal reasoning and model composition. It provides a promising avenue for improving VLM capabilities by leveraging the strengths of pre-trained LLMs. The interpretability findings could also inspire new model architectures that explicitly disentangle perception and reasoning.

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper presents a novel application of model merging across modalities, demonstrating its effectiveness in transferring reasoning abilities to VLMs. The interpretability analysis is insightful and contributes to our understanding of how VLMs work. However, the limitations in merging techniques explored, dataset specificity, and depth of hyperparameter analysis prevent it from reaching a higher score. Further, although merging models is well-known, the results could be difficult to reproduce due to dependence on the specifics of the chosen models, and potential sensitivity to hyperparameters.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### ComPO: Preference Alignment via Comparison Oracles
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05465v1)
- **Authors**: Peter Chen, Xi Chen, Wotao Yin, Tianyi Lin
- **Abstract**: Direct alignment methods are increasingly used for aligning large language models (LLMs) with human preferences. However, these methods suffer from the issues of verbosity and likelihood displacement, which can be driven by the noisy preference pairs that induce similar likelihood for preferred and dispreferred responses. The contributions of this paper are two-fold. First, we propose a new preference alignment method based on comparison oracles and provide the convergence guarantee for its basic scheme. Second, we improve our method using some heuristics and conduct the experiments to demonstrate the flexibility and compatibility of practical scheme in improving the performance of LLMs using noisy preference pairs. Evaluations are conducted across multiple base and instruction-tuned models (Mistral-7B, Llama-3-8B and Gemma-2-9B) with benchmarks (AlpacaEval 2, MT-Bench and Arena-Hard). Experimental results show the effectiveness of our method as an alternative to addressing the limitations of existing direct alignment methods. A highlight of our work is that we evidence the importance of designing specialized methods for preference pairs with distinct likelihood margin, which complements the recent findings in \citet{Razin-2025-Unintentional}.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ComPO, a new preference alignment method for Large Language Models (LLMs). ComPO leverages comparison oracles, directly utilizing comparative preference pairs to guide model parameter updates without relying on an explicit proxy objective function like Direct Preference Optimization (DPO). The authors aim to address the limitations of existing direct alignment methods, specifically verbosity and likelihood displacement, which they argue are exacerbated by noisy preference pairs (pairs inducing similar likelihoods for preferred and dispreferred responses). They provide a convergence guarantee for a basic version of their method and enhance it with heuristics for practical implementation.  Experiments across several base and instruction-tuned models and benchmarks demonstrate the effectiveness of ComPO in improving LLM performance using both clean and noisy preference data. A key highlight is the evidence presented for the importance of specialized methods for handling preference pairs with distinct likelihood margins.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using comparison oracles in preference alignment is a novel approach. While comparison oracle methods exist in optimization, adapting them to the specific challenges of LLM alignment, particularly with noisy preference data, is a unique contribution. ComPO directly leverages preference data, avoiding the complex and potentially limiting stage of explicitly defining or optimizing an objective function as a proxy for alignment. Existing methods such as DPO relies on maximizing a log-likelihood margin which can result in adverse effects. Additionally, the algorithm's use of gradient clipping and sparse gradient estimation techniques also adds to its practicality for large-scale models.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a well-known problem in LLM alignment: the sensitivity of direct preference optimization methods to noisy data. Likelihood displacement is a serious issue, as it can lead to models that actually perform *worse* after alignment. Addressing this issue has a direct impact on the trustworthiness and reliability of aligned LLMs. The results demonstrate improved length-controlled win rates and show the method alleviates the issue of verbiage. The evidence supporting the importance of handling preference pairs with varying likelihood margins is also significant, and complements recent work.
*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Sound methodology:** The theoretical convergence guarantee for the basic scheme provides a strong foundation.
    *   **Practical enhancements:** The heuristics used to improve the method's computational efficiency for large-scale model fine-tuning are well-motivated.
    *   **Empirical validation:** The experiments are comprehensive, covering multiple base and instruction-tuned models, and diverse benchmarks. The ablation study in the supplemental material examining different noise metrics is helpful.
    *   **Clear problem statement:** The paper identifies a clear problem (verbosity and likelihood displacement with noisy data) and proposes a plausible solution.
*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity of Implementation:** ComPO involves implementing the proposed modifications alongside existing methods, adding some complexity.
    *   **Dataset Dependency:** While the method is designed to work with noisy data, its overall effectiveness is still reliant on the quantity and quality of the initial preference dataset. Furthermore, performance decreases were observed on the Arena-Hard dataset due to the fact that ComPO alleviated the issue of verbiage, leading to reduced output lengths.
    *   **Limited scope:** The current implementation only micro-fine-tunes the model's output layer. While this is efficient, exploring the benefits of full-layer perturbation, even with increased computation, could be insightful.

*   **Potential Influence:** This paper has the potential to influence the direction of research in LLM alignment by highlighting the importance of directly leveraging preference comparisons and providing a practical alternative to proxy-objective optimization techniques.  The method's ability to handle noisy data is a valuable contribution to the field. Future work could focus on further simplifying the method, exploring adaptive stepsize strategies, and extending it to more challenging tasks, such as reasoning.
*   **Rigorous Rationale:** ComPO uses comparison oracles to allow models to leverage data that would not otherwise be useful in addressing the limitations of existing methods such as verbiage and likelihood displacement. Furthermore, the authors provide a convergence guarantee as well as experimentation across benchmarks, multiple models and comparisons against base models to give additional support.

**Score: 7.5**

**Rationale:** The paper demonstrates a solid contribution with a novel method, strong empirical results, and a clear direction for addressing a significant problem in LLM alignment. While the dependence on some initial datasets, output layer limited implementation, and decrease in performance over Arena-Hard datasets are limitations, the demonstrated improvements in length-controlled win rate and alleviation of lengthiness in the outputs make a valuable advance in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Mogao: An Omni Foundation Model for Interleaved Multi-Modal Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05472v1)
- **Authors**: Chao Liao, Liyang Liu, Xun Wang, Zhengxiong Luo, Xinyu Zhang, Wenliang Zhao, Jie Wu, Liang Li, Zhi Tian, Weilin Huang
- **Abstract**: Recent progress in unified models for image understanding and generation has been impressive, yet most approaches remain limited to single-modal generation conditioned on multiple modalities. In this paper, we present Mogao, a unified framework that advances this paradigm by enabling interleaved multi-modal generation through a causal approach. Mogao integrates a set of key technical improvements in architecture design, including a deep-fusion design, dual vision encoders, interleaved rotary position embeddings, and multi-modal classifier-free guidance, which allow it to harness the strengths of both autoregressive models for text generation and diffusion models for high-quality image synthesis. These practical improvements also make Mogao particularly effective to process interleaved sequences of text and images arbitrarily. To further unlock the potential of unified models, we introduce an efficient training strategy on a large-scale, in-house dataset specifically curated for joint text and image generation. Extensive experiments show that Mogao not only achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-modal understanding and text-to-image generation, but also excels in producing high-quality, coherent interleaved outputs. Its emergent capabilities in zero-shot image editing and compositional generation highlight Mogao as a practical omni-modal foundation model, paving the way for future development and scaling the unified multi-modal systems.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper, "Mogao: An Omni Foundation Model for Interleaved Multi-Modal Generation":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Mogao, a novel unified framework designed for interleaved multi-modal generation.  Unlike existing models that primarily handle single-modal generation conditioned on multiple inputs, Mogao advances the paradigm by enabling the generation of interleaved sequences of text and images. This is achieved through a causal approach and several key technical innovations, including a deep-fusion architecture, dual vision encoders (VAE and ViT), interleaved rotary position embeddings (IL-ROPE), and multi-modal classifier-free guidance. The model is trained on a large-scale, curated dataset of joint text and image data. The authors demonstrate that Mogao achieves state-of-the-art performance in multi-modal understanding, text-to-image generation, and, crucially, excels in producing high-quality, coherent interleaved outputs with emergent capabilities like zero-shot image editing and compositional generation. The goal is to provide a foundation model for future development and scaling of unified multi-modal systems.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The paper makes a solid contribution by directly addressing the challenge of interleaved multi-modal generation. While previous works have explored unified multi-modal models and hybrid AR-Diffusion architectures, Mogao distinguishes itself by its focus on generating *sequences* of intermixed text and images, rather than just single-turn outputs. The technical innovations, particularly the IL-ROPE and the dual CFG mechanism, also contribute meaningfully to the field. It is important to note that some architectural choices are inspired by existing works like MMDiT, Qwen, which reduces the novelty of the basic building blocks, but the way they are integrated for interleaving is novel.

*   **Significance:**  The potential impact of Mogao is significant. The ability to generate coherent interleaved multi-modal content has numerous applications, including content creation, interactive storytelling, and human-computer interaction. The emergent capabilities, particularly zero-shot image editing, further highlight the practical value of the model. The detailed ablation studies and comparisons to other state-of-the-art models support the claim of superior performance. The open benchmarks are a great way to show the performance of the model, especially the human evaluation.
    *   **Limitations:** While the paper demonstrates promising results, there are some limitations. One is that performance depends on a large in-house dataset. While understandable, this makes it hard for the results to be reproduced. Another limitation is the lack of further evaluations or explorations, like the amount of compute to train the model and the size of the training dataset.

*   **Clarity and Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the architecture, training strategy, and experimental results. The inclusion of visualizations is beneficial for understanding the key concepts.

*   **Rigor:** The experimental evaluation is thorough, including ablation studies to validate the effectiveness of individual components and comparisons to existing state-of-the-art models.  The use of established benchmarks and human evaluations adds further credibility to the findings.

**Justification for Score:**

Mogao represents a significant step forward in multi-modal generation. The focus on interleaved generation, combined with well-designed technical innovations and comprehensive experimental validation, makes this a valuable contribution to the field. However, the dependence on a proprietary dataset and the incremental nature of some architectural components prevents this from being at the very top of the scale. The comparison on open benchmarks makes the paper more robust and the ablation studies serve as justification.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DiffusionSfM: Predicting Structure and Motion via Ray Origin and Endpoint Diffusion
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05473v1)
- **Authors**: Qitao Zhao, Amy Lin, Jeff Tan, Jason Y. Zhang, Deva Ramanan, Shubham Tulsiani
- **Abstract**: Current Structure-from-Motion (SfM) methods typically follow a two-stage pipeline, combining learned or geometric pairwise reasoning with a subsequent global optimization step. In contrast, we propose a data-driven multi-view reasoning approach that directly infers 3D scene geometry and camera poses from multi-view images. Our framework, DiffusionSfM, parameterizes scene geometry and cameras as pixel-wise ray origins and endpoints in a global frame and employs a transformer-based denoising diffusion model to predict them from multi-view inputs. To address practical challenges in training diffusion models with missing data and unbounded scene coordinates, we introduce specialized mechanisms that ensure robust learning. We empirically validate DiffusionSfM on both synthetic and real datasets, demonstrating that it outperforms classical and learning-based approaches while naturally modeling uncertainty.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DiffusionSfM paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DiffusionSfM, a novel approach to Structure-from-Motion (SfM) that directly infers 3D scene geometry and camera poses from multi-view images using a denoising diffusion model. Unlike traditional SfM pipelines that involve pairwise reasoning followed by global optimization, DiffusionSfM unifies these stages into a single end-to-end multi-view reasoning framework. The key idea is to represent scene geometry and cameras as pixel-wise ray origins and endpoints in a global frame and then train a transformer-based denoising diffusion model to predict these elements from multi-view inputs. The paper addresses practical challenges in training diffusion models for this task by introducing techniques to handle missing data and unbounded scene coordinates.  The results demonstrate that DiffusionSfM outperforms both classical and learning-based SfM approaches on synthetic and real-world datasets while also naturally modeling uncertainty.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's core novelty lies in its use of a denoising diffusion model to directly predict dense 3D geometry and camera poses from multi-view images in an end-to-end manner.  While diffusion models have been used for pose estimation before (e.g., RayDiffusion), this paper is the first to use them to jointly predict scene structure *and* motion at a pixel-wise level, effectively replacing the traditional SfM pipeline.  The specific mechanisms introduced to handle missing data (GT mask conditioning) and unbounded scene coordinates (homogeneous coordinates) are also noteworthy contributions. The use of a two-stage sparse-to-dense training approach to better utilize the denoising diffusion model for the intended purpose is a sound idea. The architecture is solid and justified.

**Significance:**

The potential significance of this work is high.  By replacing the traditional two-stage SfM pipeline with an end-to-end learning approach, DiffusionSfM could lead to more robust and accurate 3D scene reconstruction, especially in challenging scenarios such as sparse views, textureless regions, or significant viewpoint changes. The ability to model uncertainty is also a significant advantage, as it can provide valuable information for downstream tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **End-to-End Learning:** The unified framework for structure and motion recovery is a major strength, simplifying the SfM pipeline and potentially enabling better integration of information across multiple views.
*   **Diffusion Model for Uncertainty:** The use of a diffusion model allows for the modeling of uncertainty in the predictions, which is crucial for robust SfM.
*   **Handling Missing Data and Unbounded Coordinates:** The techniques introduced to address the practical challenges of training diffusion models for this task are well-motivated and effective.
*   **Strong Empirical Results:** The paper demonstrates that DiffusionSfM outperforms existing approaches on a variety of datasets.
*   **Good Visualizations:** The qualitative results effectively show the advantages of the approach.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Computational Cost:** Diffusion models are known to be computationally expensive, especially at inference time. While the paper presents techniques to speed up inference, the computational cost remains a potential limitation.  It would have been interesting to see performance metrics comparing speed *and* accuracy using the different sampling rates, especially on larger scenes.
*   **Reliance on DINOv2:** The model relies heavily on the pre-trained DINOv2 features. While DINOv2 is a powerful feature extractor, the model's performance might be limited by the quality of these features and its transferability into different domains. It begs the question if the success of the result is from the end-to-end diffusion, or because it's using a well trained visual feature backbone (perhaps the former).
*   **Limited Evaluation of Geometry Quality:** Chamfer distance (CD) is a reasonable metric, but it might not fully capture the perceptual quality of the reconstructed geometry. Other metrics, such as normal consistency or surface smoothness, could provide a more complete evaluation.

**Potential Impact:**

DiffusionSfM has the potential to significantly impact the field of 3D vision and robotics. Its end-to-end learning approach and uncertainty modeling capabilities could lead to more robust and reliable SfM systems for a wide range of applications, including autonomous navigation, 3D mapping, and augmented reality. This method, however, has significant compute requirements, and has to be thoroughly evaluated in future works.

**Score:** 7

**Justification:**

DiffusionSfM presents a novel and promising approach to SfM using denoising diffusion models. The core idea of jointly predicting scene structure and motion at a pixel-wise level is compelling, and the technical contributions addressing the challenges of training diffusion models for this task are significant. The strong empirical results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach.

However, the computational cost, reliance on pre-trained features, and potential limitations in the evaluation of geometry quality are weaknesses that need to be addressed in future work. While the results are compelling, the computational cost of the algorithm is high, and the paper's impact is somewhat tempered by its dependence on DINOv2. Nevertheless, the paper represents a significant step forward in end-to-end SfM and has the potential to inspire further research in this area.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Prompt to Polyp: Clinically-Aware Medical Image Synthesis with Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05573v1)
- **Authors**: Mikhail Chaichuk, Sushant Gautam, Steven Hicks, Elena Tutubalina
- **Abstract**: The generation of realistic medical images from text descriptions has significant potential to address data scarcity challenges in healthcare AI while preserving patient privacy. This paper presents a comprehensive study of text-to-image synthesis in the medical domain, comparing two distinct approaches: (1) fine-tuning large pre-trained latent diffusion models and (2) training small, domain-specific models. We introduce a novel model named MSDM, an optimized architecture based on Stable Diffusion that integrates a clinical text encoder, variational autoencoder, and cross-attention mechanisms to better align medical text prompts with generated images. Our study compares two approaches: fine-tuning large pre-trained models (FLUX, Kandinsky) versus training compact domain-specific models (MSDM). Evaluation across colonoscopy (MedVQA-GI) and radiology (ROCOv2) datasets reveals that while large models achieve higher fidelity, our optimized MSDM delivers comparable quality with lower computational costs. Quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluations by medical experts reveal strengths and limitations of each approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Prompt to Polyp: Clinically-Aware Medical Image Synthesis with Diffusion Models" explores the use of diffusion models for generating medical images from text descriptions, specifically focusing on colonoscopy and radiology images. The authors compare fine-tuning large pre-trained diffusion models (FLUX and Kandinsky) with training a smaller, domain-specific model (MSDM, a variant of Stable Diffusion). They introduce MSDM and evaluate the performance of all three models across two datasets (MedVQA-GI and ROCOv2), using both quantitative metrics (FID, BiomedCLIP-based metrics) and qualitative evaluation by medical experts. The study investigates the impact of LoRA parameters and text paraphrasing on the quality of generated images. The results suggest that while larger models can achieve higher fidelity, the optimized MSDM provides comparable quality with lower computational costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper addresses a relevant problem: data scarcity in medical imaging. Generating synthetic medical images from text could be a valuable tool for augmentation, education, and privacy-preserving data sharing.
    *   The paper provides a comprehensive comparison of different approaches: fine-tuning large pre-trained models versus training domain-specific models.  This is a valuable contribution, as it allows readers to understand the tradeoffs of each approach.
    *   The introduction of MSDM, a customized architecture for medical image synthesis, is a novel contribution.  The specific architectural choices (clinical text encoder, VAE, cross-attention) are well-motivated.
    *   The use of both quantitative metrics and qualitative evaluation by medical experts is a strong point, as it provides a balanced view of model performance.  The tailored BiomedCLIP metrics improve upon standard FID scores.
    *   The exploration of LoRa fine-tuning and text paraphrasing is relevant, showing how to improve the training and performance of diffusion models in resource-constrained settings.
    *   Testing on two distinct medical imaging datasets (colonoscopy and radiology) increases the generalizability of the findings.
    *   The thorough discussion of limitations and future directions is commendable, highlighting areas for further research.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While MSDM is a novel model, it builds on existing architectures (Stable Diffusion). The incremental nature of the improvement should be acknowledged more explicitly.
    *   The quantitative results, particularly the FID scores, show some variability. While the authors acknowledge this, it makes it harder to draw definitive conclusions about the superiority of any one model.
    *   The qualitative evaluation, although valuable, relies on a single medical expert. While the expert is specified to have ample experience, a study involving multiple experts would increase the robustness and significance of the findings.
    *   The MedVQA-GI dataset has a relatively small size and limited diversity. This could limit the generalizability of the findings to other medical imaging domains. Although ROCOv2 is utilized to overcome some of these limitations, it is not clear that this data is being actively utilized for training in the current paper.
    * The paper states “dataset paraphrasing during training contributed to a 10-20 point reduction in test FID scores across all models.” This is a huge drop, which makes one question the utility of the paraphrasing strategy.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The novelty lies in the comprehensive comparison of different diffusion model architectures and training strategies in the specific context of medical image synthesis, the introduction of a specific architecture MSDM tailored for medical synthesis and thorough expert analysis. The paper also contributes a new approach to text paraphrasing for augmentation in medical image generation and evaluation in clinical domains, which is somewhat significant.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper could influence future research in medical image synthesis by providing a benchmark for comparing different diffusion model architectures and training strategies, and highlighting the importance of domain-specific adaptations.  It could also encourage the development of more sophisticated evaluation metrics that better capture clinical relevance.

**Justification for the Score:**

I am assigning a score of 7. The paper makes a solid, yet incremental, contribution to the field of medical image synthesis. It provides a thorough comparison of different diffusion models and training strategies, and introduces a novel architecture tailored for medical data. The paper has several strengths, including a comprehensive evaluation methodology, relevant datasets, and a well-written discussion of limitations and future directions. However, the variability in quantitative results, the limited number of medical experts in the qualitative evaluation, and the relatively small size and diversity of the MedVQA-GI dataset limit the significance of the findings. The drop in test FID scores due to paraphrasing is also very worrisome.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### KG-HTC: Integrating Knowledge Graphs into LLMs for Effective Zero-shot Hierarchical Text Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05583v1)
- **Authors**: Qianbo Zang, Christophe Zgrzendek, Igor Tchappi, Afshin Khadangi, Johannes Sedlmeir
- **Abstract**: Hierarchical Text Classification (HTC) involves assigning documents to labels organized within a taxonomy. Most previous research on HTC has focused on supervised methods. However, in real-world scenarios, employing supervised HTC can be challenging due to a lack of annotated data. Moreover, HTC often faces issues with large label spaces and long-tail distributions. In this work, we present Knowledge Graphs for zero-shot Hierarchical Text Classification (KG-HTC), which aims to address these challenges of HTC in applications by integrating knowledge graphs with Large Language Models (LLMs) to provide structured semantic context during classification. Our method retrieves relevant subgraphs from knowledge graphs related to the input text using a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) approach. Our KG-HTC can enhance LLMs to understand label semantics at various hierarchy levels. We evaluate KG-HTC on three open-source HTC datasets: WoS, DBpedia, and Amazon. Our experimental results show that KG-HTC significantly outperforms three baselines in the strict zero-shot setting, particularly achieving substantial improvements at deeper levels of the hierarchy. This evaluation demonstrates the effectiveness of incorporating structured knowledge into LLMs to address HTC's challenges in large label spaces and long-tailed label distributions. Our code is available at: https://github.com/QianboZang/KG-HTC.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "KG-HTC: Integrating Knowledge Graphs into LLMs for Effective Zero-shot Hierarchical Text Classification."

**Summary:**

The paper introduces KG-HTC, a novel approach for zero-shot hierarchical text classification (HTC).  The method leverages knowledge graphs (KGs) to provide structured semantic context to large language models (LLMs) during classification. KG-HTC operates by retrieving relevant subgraphs from a KG based on the input text using a Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) technique. This subgraph information is then transformed into a structured prompt and fed into an LLM to predict the hierarchical label.  The authors evaluate KG-HTC on three open-source HTC datasets (WoS, DBpedia, and Amazon) and demonstrate significant improvements over several baseline methods in a strict zero-shot setting.  The main claim is that incorporating structured knowledge from KGs enhances LLMs' ability to understand label semantics, especially at deeper levels of the hierarchy, thereby addressing the challenges of large label spaces and long-tailed distributions in HTC.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper has a decent level of novelty, but it isn't groundbreaking. The core idea of using knowledge graphs to augment LLMs is not entirely new; RAG has been used in other NLP tasks. However, the specific application of KGs within a RAG framework *specifically for zero-shot HTC* is a valuable contribution. The structured prompt engineering using upward propagation from the selected KG paths seems well-motivated for the hierarchical problem and is arguably a novel aspect. The pipeline and its architecture integrating VectorDB and GraphDB is very interesting.

**Significance:**

The significance stems from the practical challenges in HTC.  Supervised HTC is often limited by the lack of labeled data, large label spaces, and imbalanced distributions. Zero-shot HTC provides a promising solution.  The experimental results demonstrate that KG-HTC can substantially improve performance, particularly at deeper levels of the hierarchy where previous zero-shot methods struggled. This is significant because deeper levels often contain the most specific and useful information. Also, the empirical results on standard datasets and comparison with strong baselines reinforce these results and provide a solid foundation.

**Strengths:**

*   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly outlines the challenges of HTC and motivates the need for zero-shot approaches.
*   **Well-Defined Method:** The KG-HTC method is described in detail with all the components (subgraph retrieval, structured prompt generation, layer-wise classification) explained clearly. Algorithm descriptions make it replicable.
*   **Strong Empirical Evaluation:** The evaluation is thorough and uses three established HTC datasets.  The comparison against multiple baselines, including recent state-of-the-art zero-shot HTC methods (Z-STC and HiLA), strengthens the claim that KG-HTC is effective. Ablation studies further isolate the contributions of different components.
*   **Insightful Error Analysis:** The error analysis provides valuable insights into the limitations of the method, particularly the performance degradation of the RAG system at deeper levels, which offers directions for future work.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Reliance on Taxonomy Quality:** The method depends heavily on the quality and completeness of the knowledge graph (label taxonomy). The paper does not address how to handle inaccurate or missing information in the KG, which is a realistic concern in many real-world scenarios. A discussion of the cost/effort needed to build a good graph would be important.
*   **Generalizability Beyond the Datasets:** The evaluation is performed on three specific datasets. While these datasets are diverse, it is unclear how well KG-HTC would generalize to other domains or taxonomies with different characteristics. Further, details about the domains of the test set and the pre-trained knowledge sources that are used are not explained. If knowledge sources overlap with the test set, it is not purely zero-shot.
*   **Complexity:**  The KG-HTC pipeline has multiple components, including a graph database, a vector database, the RAG system, and the LLM. This adds to the complexity of implementation and may limit its adoption in resource-constrained environments. The paper is silent on this.
*   **Similarity threshold tuning:** The parameter values of the similarity threshold tuning for the RAG system are chosen experimentally. A theoretical analysis of this hyperparameter tuning needs to be done.

**Potential Influence:**

KG-HTC has the potential to influence the field by providing a practical and effective approach for zero-shot HTC. The framework could be adapted and extended to other text classification tasks that benefit from incorporating structured knowledge. The insights from the error analysis can also guide future research on improving the robustness and accuracy of RAG-based methods for HTC.

**Justification for Score:**

I'm assigning a score of **7/10**.

Rationale:

*   The paper has good novelty in the application of KGs within a RAG-based framework *specifically for zero-shot HTC* and the structured prompt engineering aspect.
*   The significance is substantial due to the practical challenges it addresses and the demonstrated improvements over existing methods.
*   The empirical evaluation is strong and provides convincing evidence for the effectiveness of KG-HTC.
*   However, the reliance on the taxonomy quality, lack of a discussion of building the initial knowledge graph, questions about generalizability, and implementation complexity limit the score. The work could benefit from more discussion of the limitations and potential ways to address them.
*   The experimental details should be more clear about the domains of the test set and knowledge graphs that are used.

In summary, the paper presents a solid contribution to the field of HTC, but it could be improved by addressing some of the identified limitations and providing a more in-depth discussion of practical considerations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 0/10

### PRIMG : Efficient LLM-driven Test Generation Using Mutant Prioritization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05584v1)
- **Authors**: Mohamed Salah Bouafif, Mohammad Hamdaqa, Edward Zulkoski
- **Abstract**: Mutation testing is a widely recognized technique for assessing and enhancing the effectiveness of software test suites by introducing deliberate code mutations. However, its application often results in overly large test suites, as developers generate numerous tests to kill specific mutants, increasing computational overhead. This paper introduces PRIMG (Prioritization and Refinement Integrated Mutation-driven Generation), a novel framework for incremental and adaptive test case generation for Solidity smart contracts. PRIMG integrates two core components: a mutation prioritization module, which employs a machine learning model trained on mutant subsumption graphs to predict the usefulness of surviving mutants, and a test case generation module, which utilizes Large Language Models (LLMs) to generate and iteratively refine test cases to achieve syntactic and behavioral correctness. We evaluated PRIMG on real-world Solidity projects from Code4Arena to assess its effectiveness in improving mutation scores and generating high-quality test cases. The experimental results demonstrate that PRIMG significantly reduces test suite size while maintaining high mutation coverage. The prioritization module consistently outperformed random mutant selection, enabling the generation of high-impact tests with reduced computational effort. Furthermore, the refining process enhanced the correctness and utility of LLM-generated tests, addressing their inherent limitations in handling edge cases and complex program logic.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces PRIMG (Prioritization and Refinement Integrated Mutation-driven Generation), a new framework designed to improve test generation for Solidity smart contracts. PRIMG combines mutation testing with Large Language Models (LLMs) to create more effective and efficient test suites. It features two key components: a mutation prioritization module that uses machine learning to select the most useful mutants for testing and a test case generation module that employs LLMs to generate and refine test cases, ensuring syntactic and behavioral correctness. The authors evaluate PRIMG on real-world Solidity projects from Code4Arena, demonstrating that it reduces test suite size while maintaining high mutation coverage and that the LLM refining process enhances test correctness.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of combining mutation testing with LLMs for smart contract test generation is not entirely novel. Prior work has explored both directions independently and some initial integrations. The novelty in PRIMG lies in the specific *integration* of a machine learning-based prioritization module with an LLM-driven test generation and refinement loop. The use of mutant subsumption graphs to train the prioritization model is a potentially interesting technique. However, the incremental contribution over existing LLM and MT driven test generation may be more of an incremental improvement. It appears the methodolgy has been tested extensively and compared to the state of the art. The study is well structured and documented, but not necessarily the most advanced or cutting edge.
* **Significance:** The paper addresses a crucial problem in smart contract development: creating effective test suites to ensure security and reliability. The potential for PRIMG to reduce test suite size and improve mutation coverage is significant, as it could reduce the computational cost and time associated with testing. Given the high stakes involved in smart contract vulnerabilities, improved testing tools have a direct positive impact. However, the authors did not compare against several methods such as the method in [4] which uses LMMS driven by mutation to improve test cases or [19] that combined LMMS with differential testing. There is a lack of a strong comparison of similar methods for effective testing. It is also notable that they did not mention any tests of the newly killed mutagens or impact on overall score. This may lead to a misunderstanding of the impact.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear problem definition:** The paper clearly articulates the need for more efficient and effective smart contract testing methods.
    * **Well-defined framework:** PRIMG is described in detail, including the roles of its two main components and the algorithms involved.
    * **Empirical evaluation:** The experiments on real-world Solidity projects from Code4Arena provide evidence of PRIMG's effectiveness.
    * **Statistical significance:** The use of statistical tests (Z-tests) to compare the performance of different configurations adds rigor to the analysis.
    * **Addresses a relevant problem**: This is particularly true for smart contracts, where security is critical.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited novelty:** As mentioned, the combination of mutation testing and LLMs is not entirely new, and the marginal contribution relative to existing integrations needs to be more clearly articulated.
    * **Limited comparison:** The paper could benefit from a more extensive comparison to existing approaches, especially other LLM-based test generation techniques and state-of-the-art test driven mutation programs. More comparison would need to be executed to evaluate and asses a true impact from this method.
    * **Generalizability to other programming languages or LMMS:** The framework is described as "fundamentally adaptable" but this claim is not tested with any secondary programming languages.
    * **Threats to Validity:** As mentioned in the paper, a key validity threat that needs to be further evaluated is the operator set of the SuMo framework.
    * **Lack of discussion about new test performance impact on overall score:** The lack of discussing impact of newly killed mutagens to the overall score could lead to a misunderstanding of the impact from testing.

* **Potential Influence:** If PRIMG proves to be a reliable and scalable testing tool, it could be adopted by smart contract developers to improve the security and quality of their code. It could also inspire further research on combining mutation testing with LLMs and other advanced techniques for software testing.

**Overall Score:**

Score: 6

**Rationale:**

PRIMG presents a potentially useful approach, but its novelty is incremental. It lacks a deep dive comparison between state of the art techniques, such as what was noted with existing work such as Dakhela et al. [4]. The experimental evaluation is thorough in its execution and structure with reasonable statistical analysis and discussion. It demonstrates practical benefits within the specific context of Solidity smart contracts; however the approach has significant validity threats from generalizability to other programming languages or LMMS. The framework provides a strong step in LMMS based testing, but needs a stronger assessment for broad real world application.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### ReactDance: Progressive-Granular Representation for Long-Term Coherent Reactive Dance Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05589v1)
- **Authors**: Jingzhong Lin, Yuanyuan Qi, Xinru Li, Wenxuan Huang, Xiangfeng Xu, Bangyan Li, Xuejiao Wang, Gaoqi He
- **Abstract**: Reactive dance generation (RDG) produces follower movements conditioned on guiding dancer and music while ensuring spatial coordination and temporal coherence. However, existing methods overemphasize global constraints and optimization, overlooking local information, such as fine-grained spatial interactions and localized temporal context. Therefore, we present ReactDance, a novel diffusion-based framework for high-fidelity RDG with long-term coherence and multi-scale controllability. Unlike existing methods that struggle with interaction fidelity, synchronization, and temporal consistency in duet synthesis, our approach introduces two key innovations: 1)Group Residual Finite Scalar Quantization (GRFSQ), a multi-scale disentangled motion representation that captures interaction semantics from coarse body rhythms to fine-grained joint dynamics, and 2)Blockwise Local Context (BLC), a sampling strategy eliminating error accumulation in long sequence generation via local block causal masking and periodic positional encoding. Built on the decoupled multi-scale GRFSQ representation, we implement a diffusion model withLayer-Decoupled Classifier-free Guidance (LDCFG), allowing granular control over motion semantics across scales. Extensive experiments on standard benchmarks demonstrate that ReactDance surpasses existing methods, achieving state-of-the-art performance.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "ReactDance: Progressive-Granular Representation for Long-Term Coherent Reactive Dance Generation" introduces a new framework for generating reactive dance movements, where a follower's dance is conditioned on the motion of a leader and music. The framework aims to improve upon existing methods that struggle with interaction fidelity, synchronization, and long-term consistency. The key innovations are: 1) Group Residual Finite Scalar Quantization (GRFSQ), a multi-scale representation capturing motion semantics from coarse body rhythms to fine-grained joint dynamics; 2) Blockwise Local Context (BLC), a sampling strategy for eliminating error accumulation in long sequences via local causal masking; and 3) Layer-Decoupled Classifier-Free Guidance (LDCFG), enabling granular control over motion semantics across scales using a diffusion model. The authors claim state-of-the-art performance on standard benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of techniques, some of which are inspired by or adapted from prior work.  The GRFSQ representation, building on FSQ, appears to be a significant contribution in terms of its hierarchical structure and disentanglement of motion semantics at different granularities. BLC and LDCFG, while valuable additions, are arguably adaptations of existing concepts to the specific problem of reactive dance generation. The claim of a new sampling strategy in BLC could be considered novel, yet it depends on the uniqueness compared to other causal masking approaches. The combined architecture of GRFSQ, BLC, and LDCFG provides a strong foundation for generating high-quality reactive dance.

*   **Significance:**  Reactive dance generation is a challenging problem with applications in virtual avatars, digital interaction, and entertainment. The paper tackles key issues such as interaction fidelity and temporal coherence. The ability to generate long, coherent dance sequences that are synchronized with both leader motion and music is a valuable contribution. Quantitative and qualitative results are strong indicators of the method's success. However, the user study, although present, has very low sample rate.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The GRFSQ representation seems well-designed to capture the hierarchical nature of dance movements, from coarse body rhythms to fine-grained joint dynamics.
    *   The BLC strategy addresses the critical problem of error accumulation in long sequence generation, a common issue in autoregressive models.
    *   The LDCFG approach provides fine-grained control over motion semantics, allowing for better control over the style and characteristics of the generated dance.
    *   The authors provide a comprehensive set of quantitative evaluation metrics, including FID, Diversity, BED, and BAS. User studies also contribute to the evaluation of the perceptual quality of the generated dances.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The novelty of the BLC and LDCFG components could be stronger. The paper needs to clearly highlight the distinction from existing causal masking and classifier-free guidance methods.
    *   The computational complexity and training requirements are not sufficiently discussed. The paper mentions that the framework is implemented on a single NVIDIA RTX 4090 GPU and trained with certain configurations but lacks specific details about the training time and resource consumption. This could be a limiting factor for wider adoption.
    *   The evaluation on DD100 dataset is limited. A more exhaustive ablation study would have strengthened the analysis of the individual contributions of each component.
    *   The reliance on a dataset (DD100) limits the generalizability of the research, especially given that the dataset is only from 10 musical genres.

*   **Potential Influence:** If the code and models are released, the paper has the potential to influence research in reactive dance generation, motion synthesis, and virtual avatars. The GRFSQ representation could be adopted by other researchers working on motion modeling. The BLC strategy could be useful for other long sequence generation tasks.

*   **Justification of Score:** While the paper addresses a relevant problem and introduces a well-designed framework, the novelty of individual components is not groundbreaking, but rather, the combination of techniques and the GRFSQ seems novel. The experimental results are promising, but further details on computational aspects and a broader ablation study would strengthen the analysis. Given these factors, I assign the paper a score of 7.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Enhancing Large Language Models with Faster Code Preprocessing for Vulnerability Detection
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05600v1)
- **Authors**: José Gonçalves, Miguel Silva, Eva Maia, Isabel Praça
- **Abstract**: The application of Artificial Intelligence has become a powerful approach to detecting software vulnerabilities. However, effective vulnerability detection relies on accurately capturing the semantic structure of code and its contextual relationships. Given that the same functionality can be implemented in various forms, a preprocessing tool that standardizes code representation is important. This tool must be efficient, adaptable across programming languages, and capable of supporting new transformations. To address this challenge, we build on the existing SCoPE framework and introduce SCoPE2, an enhanced version with improved performance. We compare both versions in terms of processing time and memory usage and evaluate their impact on a Large Language Model (LLM) for vulnerability detection. Our results show a 97.3\% reduction in processing time with SCoPE2, along with an improved F1-score for the LLM, solely due to the refined preprocessing approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces SCOPE2, an enhanced version of the SCOPE framework, designed for more efficient and flexible code preprocessing for software vulnerability detection (SVD).  SCOPE2 addresses limitations in the original SCOPE (specifically around parsing speed and handling of code containing macros) by using TreeSitter instead of ANTLR4. The authors demonstrate SCOPE2's performance improvements in terms of processing time and memory usage, showing a substantial reduction in processing time compared to the original SCOPE. They also fine-tune a Large Language Model (LLM), LLaMA 3.2, using data preprocessed with SCOPE2 and compare its vulnerability detection performance with data processed by SCOPE, showing a slight improvement in precision and overall F1 score. The authors claim that the enhanced performance stems from better inclusion of diverse code constructs, leading to a more accurate representation and improved performance of the LLM model when detecting vulnerabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The main novelty lies in the improvements made to the existing SCOPE framework to create SCOPE2.  Switching from ANTLR4 to TreeSitter to enhance efficiency and handle macros is a tangible technical contribution. The application of SCOPE2 to fine-tune LLaMA 3.2 and comparing the results with the original SCOPE isn't radically new, but it provides empirical evidence of the benefits.  The individual techniques (LLMs for SVD, code preprocessing) are not novel in isolation; the novelty is in the *combination* and the *specific implementation* of the improved preprocessing tool.

*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on the practical value of improving code preprocessing tools. While using LLMs for SVD is an active research area, effective preprocessing is crucial for improving both the efficiency and accuracy of LLM based vulnerability detection methods. A faster, more flexible tool that can efficiently preprocess larger and more diverse codebases is useful. The improvement in the LLM's performance, while modest, points to the benefits of better data preparation. However, the authors should have explored a wider number of LLMs or more extensive hyperparameters to showcase more substantial impacts when paired with SCOPE2. The limitation in that respect reduces the overall impact on the field.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined goals.
    *   Detailed explanation of the improvements in SCOPE2.
    *   Empirical evaluation demonstrating performance gains.
    *   Open-source implementation makes the tool accessible.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The improvement in the LLM's F1-score is rather small, making the impact somewhat questionable.
    *   The evaluation is limited to a single LLM (LLaMA 3.2) with hyperparametes from another study. A broader evaluation across different LLMs or more extensive hyperparameter tuning on LLaMA could have strengthened the conclusions.
    *   Lack of comparison with *other* existing code preprocessing tools. Benchmarking against alternative approaches would better contextualize the contribution.
    *   Discussion of Threats to Validity could be more extensive (e.g., discussing biases in DiverseVul, the potential for overfitting, etc).

*   **Potential Impact:** While the current impact seems limited, the open-source release of SCOPE2 makes it a potentially valuable tool for researchers working on LLM-based vulnerability detection. It could serve as a building block for more advanced preprocessing techniques and workflows. It addresses a practical problem, even if the measured effect in this paper is modest.

*   **Overall:** The paper presents a solid engineering contribution with tangible benefits. The improved preprocessing tool addresses a practical problem, and the empirical evaluation provides evidence of its effectiveness. However, the relatively small improvement in the LLM's F1-score, the limited evaluation scope, and the absence of comparison with other preprocessing tools, weakens the overall impact and prevent a high score.

**Score: 6**

**Justification:**

A score of 6 reflects the positive aspects of the work (improved preprocessing tool, clear evaluation), while acknowledging the limitations (modest LLM performance improvement, limited comparison with other methods). The work is incrementally novel and useful, but it doesn't represent a breakthrough or significantly shift the state of the art in LLM based vulnerability detection. The significance is present but is limited by the narrow experimental scope.

- **Classification**: cs.SE
- **Score**: 6/10

### HiBayES: A Hierarchical Bayesian Modeling Framework for AI Evaluation Statistics
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05602v1)
- **Authors**: Lennart Luettgau, Harry Coppock, Magda Dubois, Christopher Summerfield, Cozmin Ududec
- **Abstract**: As Large Language Models (LLMs) and other AI systems evolve, robustly estimating their capabilities from inherently stochastic outputs while systematically quantifying uncertainty in these estimates becomes increasingly important. Further, advanced AI evaluations often have a nested hierarchical structure, exhibit high levels of complexity, and come with high costs in testing the most advanced AI systems. To address these challenges, we introduce HiBayES, a generalizable Hierarchical Bayesian modeling framework for AI Evaluation Statistics. HiBayES supports robust inferences in classical question-answer benchmarks and advanced agentic evaluations, particularly in low-data scenarios (e.g., < 20 data points per evaluation). Built on Generalized Linear Models (GLMs), Bayesian data analysis, and formal model comparison, HiBayES provides principled uncertainty quantification and robust parameter estimation. This paper offers a comprehensive introduction to HiBayES, including illustrative examples, comparisons to conventional statistical methods, and practical guidance for implementing multilevel Bayesian GLMs. Additionally, we provide a HiBayES software package [4] (Beta version) for out-of-the-box implementation.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces HiBayES, a hierarchical Bayesian modeling framework designed for AI evaluation statistics, particularly for large language models (LLMs) and agentic systems. HiBayES aims to address limitations in conventional evaluation methods by providing principled uncertainty quantification, explicitly modeling the hierarchical structure of evaluation data, and enabling robust inference in low-data regimes. The framework is built upon Generalized Linear Models (GLMs), Bayesian data analysis, and formal model comparison techniques. The paper presents illustrative examples, comparisons to traditional statistical approaches (like t-tests), and practical guidance for implementing multilevel Bayesian GLMs.  It also includes a HiBayES software package. The authors argue that HiBayES offers more reliable, nuanced, and scientifically sound evaluation of AI capabilities, especially in the context of expensive and complex evaluations with limited data. They demonstrate its utility in scenarios like estimating overall performance, comparing models across domains, and capability elicitation in agentic benchmarks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a real need:** The paper directly tackles a growing problem in AI evaluation – the limitations of traditional statistical methods when dealing with complex, hierarchical data and low-data regimes, which are increasingly common due to the cost of evaluating advanced AI systems. The paper clearly outlines the shortcomings of existing methods, particularly the overconfidence in results and failure to account for data structure.
    *   **Principled approach:** HiBayES offers a theoretically sound alternative based on Bayesian GLMs, known for their ability to handle uncertainty and model complex data dependencies. The use of hierarchical modeling is especially valuable for capturing the nested structure inherent in many AI evaluation datasets (LLMs, domains, subdomains, items, repeats).
    *   **Practical guidance and software:** The paper provides clear and comprehensive guidance on implementing multilevel Bayesian GLMs in the context of AI evaluation, including illustrative examples and a software package. This makes the framework more accessible to researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Formal model comparison:** The emphasis on formal model comparison using information criteria (WAIC) is a significant improvement over simply reporting point estimates and p-values. This allows for a more rigorous assessment of which data-generating process best explains the observed data.
    *   **Thorough comparison and decision support:** Table 1 provides an extremely valuable comparison of the trade offs of HiBayes and more classical statistical approaches, and figure 1 provides a clear decision tree of how to decide which approach best fits the use case.
    *   **Addresses the issues within agentic benchmarks:** The approach appears particularly useful for evaluating agentic systems where the relationship between reasoning/scaffolding effort and performance can be complex and data collection is often expensive.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Increased Complexity:** While a strength, the Bayesian approach is more complex than conventional statistics. This requires a greater level of statistical expertise to implement and interpret correctly. While the authors offer practical guidance, there's a learning curve for those unfamiliar with Bayesian methods.
    *   **Prior specification:** Bayesian methods require specifying prior distributions, which can be subjective. While the authors discuss this and suggest strategies for mitigating potential biases (e.g., using mildly informative priors), the choice of priors can still influence the results.
    *   **Scalability:** While HiBayES claims to be stable in low-data regimes, there may be scalability challenges with very large and complex datasets, particularly when dealing with computationally intensive MCMC sampling. The paper does not discuss potential solutions for improving scalability.
    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** Although the paper provides illustrative examples, more extensive empirical validation across a wider range of AI evaluation tasks would strengthen the claims. A comparison against other advanced statistical methods (e.g., mixed-effects models using frequentist approaches) could also be beneficial.
    *   **Beta status of HiBayes software:** Acknowledging that the HiBayes software package is in beta, its functionality and stability may be limited at this stage.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   HiBayES represents a significant step forward in AI evaluation methodology. While Bayesian methods and hierarchical modeling are not new in statistics, their application to AI evaluation statistics, particularly within a comprehensive framework like HiBayES, is relatively novel.
    *   The framework's emphasis on uncertainty quantification, explicit modeling of data structure, and formal model comparison addresses critical gaps in existing approaches.
    *   The potential impact of HiBayES is substantial, as it could lead to more reliable and accurate assessments of AI capabilities, better-informed decision-making, and improved safety and safeguards in deployment.

**Justification for the Score:**

I assign a score of 7.5.

*   It addresses a critical need in the field of AI evaluation with a principled and theoretically sound approach.
*   The practical guidance and software package enhance accessibility and facilitate adoption.
*   The framework's emphasis on uncertainty quantification, formal model comparison, and explicit modeling of data structure are significant improvements over conventional methods.
*   While the increased complexity and the need for prior specification are limitations, the authors offer mitigation strategies and provide a clear framework for implementation.
*   The beta status of the software and the need for further empirical validation slightly reduce the overall score.

Score: 7.5

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### scDrugMap: Benchmarking Large Foundation Models for Drug Response Prediction
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05612v1)
- **Authors**: Qing Wang, Yining Pan, Minghao Zhou, Zijia Tang, Yanfei Wang, Guangyu Wang, Qianqian Song
- **Abstract**: Drug resistance presents a major challenge in cancer therapy. Single cell profiling offers insights into cellular heterogeneity, yet the application of large-scale foundation models for predicting drug response in single cell data remains underexplored. To address this, we developed scDrugMap, an integrated framework featuring both a Python command-line interface and a web server for drug response prediction. scDrugMap evaluates a wide range of foundation models, including eight single-cell models and two large language models, using a curated dataset of over 326,000 cells in the primary collection and 18,800 cells in the validation set, spanning 36 datasets and diverse tissue and cancer types. We benchmarked model performance under pooled-data and cross-data evaluation settings, employing both layer freezing and Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) fine-tuning strategies. In the pooled-data scenario, scFoundation achieved the best performance, with mean F1 scores of 0.971 (layer freezing) and 0.947 (fine-tuning), outperforming the lowest-performing model by over 50%. In the cross-data setting, UCE excelled post fine-tuning (mean F1: 0.774), while scGPT led in zero-shot learning (mean F1: 0.858). Overall, scDrugMap provides the first large-scale benchmark of foundation models for drug response prediction in single-cell data and serves as a user-friendly, flexible platform for advancing drug discovery and translational research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces scDrugMap, a comprehensive framework for benchmarking large-scale foundation models in the context of drug response prediction using single-cell data. The framework includes a curated dataset of single-cell transcriptomes with drug response labels, a computational pipeline for evaluating various foundation models (both single-cell specific and general-purpose language models), and an interactive web server for easy access and analysis.  The study benchmarks ten foundation models using pooled-data and cross-data evaluation strategies, also analyzing the computational scalability of each foundation model. The key finding is that scFoundation consistently performs well in pooled-data evaluations, while UCE and scGPT excel in cross-data scenarios. The paper emphasizes the value of fine-tuning for achieving optimal performance and provides a valuable resource for researchers in drug discovery and translational research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper provides a rigorous and comprehensive benchmarking of a wide range of foundation models for drug response prediction, which is a relatively unexplored area. This addresses a crucial gap in the literature.
    *   **Resource Creation:** The development of scDrugMap and its curated dataset is a significant contribution. This provides a valuable and readily accessible resource for the community, facilitating future research and model development.
    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The use of pooled-data and cross-data evaluation strategies provides a robust assessment of model performance and generalizability, simulating both ideal and real-world scenarios.
    *   **Scalability Analysis:** Evaluating the computational efficiency and scalability of the models is crucial for practical deployment and large-scale single-cell analysis, and it is an important aspect that this study covers.
    *   **Accessibility:** The development of a user-friendly web server enhances the accessibility and usability of the framework, making it easier for researchers with varying levels of computational expertise to leverage its capabilities.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Novelty in Methods:** While the application is novel, the individual components, such as using LoRA for fine-tuning and basic machine learning metrics, are not particularly innovative on their own.
    *   **General Purpose LLMs' Poor Performance:** The poor performance of general purpose LLMs is somewhat expected, and doesn't provide substantial new knowledge.
    *   **Lack of In-Depth Biological Interpretation:** The paper focuses more on benchmarking and model performance metrics and less on providing detailed biological insights derived from the models' predictions. A deeper dive into the biological relevance of the findings could significantly enhance the paper's impact.
    *   **Dataset Curation Bias:** Though mentioned, the potential biases introduced during the creation of the dataset via literature search require greater discussion.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper directly addresses a critical need for evaluating and comparing foundation models in the context of drug response prediction.
    *   The study provides a foundation for future research in this area, guiding model selection and development efforts.
    *   The scDrugMap framework has the potential to accelerate drug discovery and translational research by providing a readily accessible and user-friendly platform for analyzing single-cell data.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution to the field by providing a comprehensive benchmarking platform for foundation models in drug response prediction. The creation of scDrugMap and its curated dataset fills a gap in the resources available to researchers working with single-cell data. The systematic evaluation and scalability analysis provide valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of different models.

However, the novelty of the methods is not exceptional, and the poor performance of general-purpose LLMs is not surprising. Additionally, a deeper exploration of the biological implications of the findings would have further strengthened the paper. While impactful, scDrugMap primarily integrates existing methods, and the resulting analyses/benchmarking are more of a valuable service to the field rather than a groundbreaking contribution of scientific knowledge.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Leveraging Large Language Models for enzymatic reaction prediction and characterization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05616v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Di Fruscia, Jana Marie Weber
- **Abstract**: Predicting enzymatic reactions is crucial for applications in biocatalysis, metabolic engineering, and drug discovery, yet it remains a complex and resource-intensive task. Large Language Models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated remarkable success in various scientific domains, e.g., through their ability to generalize knowledge, reason over complex structures, and leverage in-context learning strategies. In this study, we systematically evaluate the capability of LLMs, particularly the Llama-3.1 family (8B and 70B), across three core biochemical tasks: Enzyme Commission number prediction, forward synthesis, and retrosynthesis. We compare single-task and multitask learning strategies, employing parameter-efficient fine-tuning via LoRA adapters. Additionally, we assess performance across different data regimes to explore their adaptability in low-data settings. Our results demonstrate that fine-tuned LLMs capture biochemical knowledge, with multitask learning enhancing forward- and retrosynthesis predictions by leveraging shared enzymatic information. We also identify key limitations, for example challenges in hierarchical EC classification schemes, highlighting areas for further improvement in LLM-driven biochemical modeling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the ability of Large Language Models (LLMs), specifically the Llama-3.1 family, to perform enzymatic reaction prediction and characterization across three core biochemical tasks: Enzyme Commission (EC) number prediction, forward synthesis, and retrosynthesis. The authors compare single-task and multitask learning strategies using parameter-efficient fine-tuning via LoRA adapters. They also assess the performance of LLMs in low-data settings. The results suggest that fine-tuned LLMs can capture biochemical knowledge, and multitask learning can enhance forward- and retrosynthesis predictions. The paper also identifies limitations, such as challenges in hierarchical EC classification.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its systematic evaluation of a *specific* LLM family (Llama-3.1) across a *defined set* of core biochemical tasks (EC number prediction, forward synthesis, and retrosynthesis). While LLMs have been applied to chemistry before, this work contributes a focused analysis of their capabilities in the *enzymatic reaction* domain, considering different learning strategies (single-task vs. multitask) and data regimes.  The explicit comparison of single-task and multi-task fine-tuning strategies in this specific context adds to the paper's novelty.  It's also somewhat novel to specifically look at the use of Tanimoto similarity to evaluate 'wrong' answers, as this provides insight into potentially useful alternative reactions.

**Significance:** The paper demonstrates that LLMs, when fine-tuned, *can* perform reasonably well on these biochemical tasks. This is significant because it suggests a path towards using general-purpose models to accelerate biochemical research, potentially reducing the reliance on specialized models and extensive domain-specific expertise. The finding that multitask learning can enhance performance on forward- and retrosynthesis provides a valuable insight for future research.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a systematic evaluation of the Llama-3.1 family across multiple tasks and under varying conditions.
*   **Detailed Analysis:** The paper delves into specific challenges and limitations of LLMs in this domain, such as hierarchical EC classification, and the use of Tanimoto similarity is a nice touch.
