*   **Reproducibility:** The authors provide a link to the code and data, promoting reproducibility.
*   **Careful Data Preparation:** The paper emphasizes the importance of data preprocessing to avoid leakage and biases.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Scope:** The study primarily focuses on the BRENDA subset of the ECREACT dataset, limiting the generalizability of the findings and making direct comparisons to SOTA models trained on the full ECREACT dataset difficult. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
*   **Lack of Groundbreaking Performance:**  The LLMs, even after fine-tuning, do not consistently outperform specialized models or establish new state-of-the-art results. The authors admit the compared SOTA retains a significant edge across all levels. The main strength is demonstrating viability, not achieving superior accuracy.
*   **Dataset Preprocessing Simplification:** The ECREACT dataset, even in the BRENDA subset, is also preprocessed and simplifies complex biochemical reaction mechanisms, potentially masking difficulties that may arise in real-world applications.
*   **Data Leakage Concerns:** Despite efforts to prevent data leakage, the authors acknowledge the possibility that the models may have been exposed to similar data during pretraining. It's challenging to completely eliminate this possibility, but more rigorous efforts to use truly held-out data would strengthen the findings.
*   **Incremental Contribution:** While systematic, the application of LLMs to this area is becoming more common. The gains are incremental rather than revolutionary.

**Overall:**

The paper makes a solid, but not groundbreaking, contribution to the field by systematically evaluating the performance of Llama-3.1 models on core enzymatic reaction prediction tasks. The insights into multitask learning and the limitations of LLMs in hierarchical classification are valuable. However, the limited scope, lack of SOTA performance, and concerns about data leakage prevent the paper from being truly transformative.

**Score: 6/10**

**Rationale:** The paper is a well-executed but incremental contribution. It provides valuable insights into applying LLMs to biochemistry, but it does not present a paradigm shift or achieve state-of-the-art performance. The identified weaknesses, particularly the limited scope and lack of conclusive evidence of superior performance, prevent a higher score.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### LiteLMGuard: Seamless and Lightweight On-Device Prompt Filtering for Safeguarding Small Language Models against Quantization-induced Risks and Vulnerabilities
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05619v1)
- **Authors**: Kalyan Nakka, Jimmy Dani, Ausmit Mondal, Nitesh Saxena
- **Abstract**: The growing adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) has influenced the development of their lighter counterparts-Small Language Models (SLMs)-to enable on-device deployment across smartphones and edge devices. These SLMs offer enhanced privacy, reduced latency, server-free functionality, and improved user experience. However, due to resource constraints of on-device environment, SLMs undergo size optimization through compression techniques like quantization, which can inadvertently introduce fairness, ethical and privacy risks. Critically, quantized SLMs may respond to harmful queries directly, without requiring adversarial manipulation, raising significant safety and trust concerns. To address this, we propose LiteLMGuard (LLMG), an on-device prompt guard that provides real-time, prompt-level defense for quantized SLMs. Additionally, our prompt guard is designed to be model-agnostic such that it can be seamlessly integrated with any SLM, operating independently of underlying architectures. Our LLMG formalizes prompt filtering as a deep learning (DL)-based prompt answerability classification task, leveraging semantic understanding to determine whether a query should be answered by any SLM. Using our curated dataset, Answerable-or-Not, we trained and fine-tuned several DL models and selected ELECTRA as the candidate, with 97.75% answerability classification accuracy. Our safety effectiveness evaluations demonstrate that LLMG defends against over 87% of harmful prompts, including both direct instruction and jailbreak attack strategies. We further showcase its ability to mitigate the Open Knowledge Attacks, where compromised SLMs provide unsafe responses without adversarial prompting. In terms of prompt filtering effectiveness, LLMG achieves near state-of-the-art filtering accuracy of 94%, with an average latency of 135 ms, incurring negligible overhead for users.
- **Summary**: Okay, I will provide a summary and critical evaluation of the paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "LiteLMGuard: Seamless and Lightweight On-Device Prompt Filtering for Safeguarding Small Language Models against Quantization-induced Risks and Vulnerabilities" addresses the security risks introduced when Small Language Models (SLMs) are quantized for on-device deployment. Quantization, while reducing model size and improving efficiency, can compromise safety by making SLMs more susceptible to generating harmful content. The paper proposes LiteLMGuard, an on-device prompt guard that filters user queries in real-time to prevent potentially harmful prompts from being processed by the SLM. LiteLMGuard is designed to be model-agnostic and lightweight, ensuring minimal impact on performance and user experience. The system uses a deep learning (DL) model (specifically, ELECTRA) trained to classify prompts as "answerable" or "not answerable," with harmful or risky prompts categorized as "not answerable" and thus blocked. The paper presents evaluations demonstrating LiteLMGuard's effectiveness in blocking harmful prompts, including those generated by jailbreak attacks, and showcases its performance in terms of latency and accuracy on various mobile devices. Furthermore, the paper introduces the concept of "Open Knowledge Attacks" a novel threat model that leverages publicly available compromised SLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a somewhat novel approach to securing SLMs in resource-constrained environments. While prompt filtering is not entirely new, the focus on *on-device* deployment, the *lightweight* design constraint, and the specific vulnerability induced by *quantization* differentiate this work. The "Open Knowledge Attack" threat model is also a valuable contribution, highlighting a realistic and scalable threat vector.
*   **Significance:** The work is significant because it directly addresses a growing concern in the field of AI: the potential for SLMs to generate harmful content, especially when deployed on edge devices where computational resources are limited and security is paramount. The on-device nature of LiteLMGuard is crucial for privacy-sensitive applications and scenarios with intermittent network connectivity. The evaluations are comprehensive, providing concrete evidence of the system's effectiveness in various settings and against different attack strategies.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper focuses on a practical problem with real-world implications. The design and implementation of LiteLMGuard prioritize deployability and efficiency.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper includes thorough evaluations of both safety effectiveness and prompt filtering performance, using multiple datasets, prompting strategies, and devices.
    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly defines the problem of quantization-induced vulnerabilities and proposes a targeted solution.
    *   **Open Knowledge Attack Threat Model:** This novel framing highlights a realistic and scalable threat, adding to the paper's contribution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Supervised Learning:** The performance of LiteLMGuard heavily relies on the quality and representativeness of the "Answerable-or-Not" dataset. The paper acknowledges this limitation, but it is a critical factor to consider, especially as the landscape of adversarial prompts and SLM vulnerabilities is constantly evolving. While a continuous training regime is mentioned as future work, it does not mitigate the fact that this specific implementation may be vulnerable to data shift problems that many supervised approaches tend to suffer from.
    *   **Limited Scope of "Harmful" Prompts:** While the paper uses a safety taxonomy, the specific types of harmful prompts considered might not cover all possible risks. The effectiveness against more subtle or nuanced attacks could be further explored. The current implementation is likely limited by the scope of the safety taxonomy used in the initial data-gathering processes.
    *   **Simplistic Classification Task:** The "answerable" or "not answerable" framing can be too simplistic. Some prompts might be partially answerable or require a more nuanced assessment of risk.
    *   **Generalizability:** The choice of ELECTRA as the classification model, while effective, might not be optimal for all SLMs or edge device configurations. The generalizability of the approach to other language models and architectures requires further investigation.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper's findings could influence the design and development of safety mechanisms for SLMs deployed on edge devices. It could also motivate further research into more robust and adaptive prompt filtering techniques. The "Open Knowledge Attack" threat model could inform the development of strategies for detecting and mitigating malicious SLMs distributed through open-source repositories.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and practical contribution to the field. The novelty lies in the specific combination of on-device deployment, lightweight design, and addressing quantization-induced vulnerabilities. The evaluations are comprehensive and convincing, demonstrating the effectiveness of LiteLMGuard in various settings. However, the reliance on a supervised learning approach with a potentially limited dataset and a relatively simplistic classification task slightly limits the overall impact and robustness of the proposed solution. The proposed solution is very much tied to the supervised training regime.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CR
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Preliminary Study for GPT-4o on Image Restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05621v1)
- **Authors**: Hao Yang, Yan Yang, Ruikun Zhang, Liyuan Pan
- **Abstract**: OpenAI's GPT-4o model, integrating multi-modal inputs and outputs within an autoregressive architecture, has demonstrated unprecedented performance in image generation. In this work, we investigate its potential impact on the image restoration community. We present the first systematic evaluation of GPT-4o across diverse restoration tasks. Our experiments reveal that, although restoration outputs from GPT-4o are visually appealing, they often suffer from pixel-level structural fidelity when compared to ground-truth images. Common issues are variations in image proportions, shifts in object positions and quantities, and changes in viewpoint.To address it, taking image dehazing, derainning, and low-light enhancement as representative case studies, we show that GPT-4o's outputs can serve as powerful visual priors, substantially enhancing the performance of existing dehazing networks. It offers practical guidelines and a baseline framework to facilitate the integration of GPT-4o into future image restoration pipelines. We hope the study on GPT-4o image restoration will accelerate innovation in the broader field of image generation areas. To support further research, we will release GPT-4o-restored images from over 10 widely used image restoration datasets.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

This paper presents an initial exploration of OpenAI's GPT-4o model for various image restoration tasks. The authors conduct a systematic evaluation across diverse restoration problems and find that while GPT-4o generates visually appealing results, it often struggles with pixel-level structural fidelity. Common issues include distorted image proportions, incorrect object placement and quantity, and inconsistent viewpoints. To mitigate these limitations, the authors propose a baseline framework where GPT-4o's outputs are used as visual priors to enhance the performance of existing restoration networks.  They focus on image dehazing as a case study, demonstrating improvements using Restormer as a post-processing network, guided by GPT-4o. The paper also publicly releases GPT-4o-restored images from over 10 image restoration datasets to encourage further research.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**

The paper's novelty lies primarily in being the *first* systematic evaluation of OpenAI's GPT-4o specifically for the task of image restoration. While the idea of using large multimodal models for image generation and restoration is not entirely new, the investigation into GPT-4o's capabilities and limitations in this context is a timely and relevant contribution. The proposed baseline approach, while simple, offers a pragmatic method to integrate GPT-4o into existing restoration pipelines.

However, the novelty of the proposed method itself (combining GPT-4o with Restormer) is limited.  Restormer is a well-established architecture, and the fusion strategy (element-wise addition) is basic.  The paper's innovation is less about inventing a new restoration technique and more about *analyzing and adapting* an existing, powerful model for a specific task.

**Significance:**

The paper's significance stems from its potential to influence the image restoration community's approach to leveraging large multimodal models. By highlighting both the strengths (visual appeal, generalizability) and weaknesses (structural infidelity) of GPT-4o, the authors provide valuable insights and guide future research directions. The public release of the GPT-4o-restored dataset is also a significant contribution, enabling other researchers to build upon their findings.  The paper's analysis of GPT-4o's failure cases, specifically the issues with spatial consistency, is particularly useful.

However, the significance is somewhat tempered by the relatively simple baseline approach.  While the results show improvements, they aren't groundbreaking and may not be generalizable across all restoration tasks. A more sophisticated fusion strategy or a more targeted approach for specific degradation types could have demonstrated greater impact.  The reliance on CLIP-IQA as the primary metric for perceptual quality is also a weakness; CLIP-IQA is known to sometimes correlate poorly with human perception in image restoration tasks.

**Strengths:**

*   **Timely and Relevant:** Addresses a current trend in machine learning.
*   **Systematic Evaluation:** Provides a thorough investigation of GPT-4o across diverse tasks.
*   **Practical Baseline:** Offers a readily implementable framework for integrating GPT-4o.
*   **Public Dataset:** Contributes a valuable resource to the community.
*   **Clear Identification of Limitations:** Points out important challenges and future research directions.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Method:** The baseline approach is relatively simple.
*   **Modest Improvements:** The performance gains, while positive, aren't revolutionary.
*   **Reliance on CLIP-IQA:**  The use of CLIP-IQA heavily influences perception, potentially skewing perception of output quality.
*   **Limited Analysis of Computational Cost:** The paper mentions computational cost but doesn't provide a detailed comparison with existing methods, which is a crucial factor for practical applications. The 82s inference time of GPT-4o per image is a significant limitation not sufficiently addressed.

**Score:**

Based on the above analysis, I would assign this paper a score of **6**.

**Rationale:**

While the paper offers a valuable and timely first look at GPT-4o for image restoration, its limited methodological novelty and the modest improvements achieved hold it back from a higher score. The primary contribution is in the analysis, identification of limitations, and the release of a dataset. The practical framework is a nice addition, but the approach itself is straightforward. The paper highlights important challenges and provides a foundation for future research, but doesn't represent a significant leap forward in image restoration techniques. A deeper dive into the computational cost, a more sophisticated fusion strategy, and a more compelling demonstration of practical benefits would have warranted a higher score. The score reflects the paper's importance as an early exploration and its potential to guide future work, tempered by its relatively incremental contributions to the state-of-the-art.
Score: 6

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 6/10

### Looking Beyond Language Priors: Enhancing Visual Comprehension and Attention in Multimodal Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05626v1)
- **Authors**: Aarti Ghatkesar, Uddeshya Upadhyay, Ganesh Venkatesh
- **Abstract**: Achieving deep alignment between vision and language remains a central challenge for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). These models often fail to fully leverage visual input, defaulting to strong language priors. Our approach first provides insights into how MLLMs internally build visual understanding of image regions and then introduces techniques to amplify this capability. Specifically, we explore techniques designed both to deepen the model's understanding of visual content and to ensure that these visual insights actively guide language generation. We demonstrate the superior multimodal understanding of our resultant model through a detailed upstream analysis quantifying its ability to predict visually-dependent tokens as well as 10 pt boost on visually challenging tasks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a novelty/significance score:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of weak visual grounding in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  It argues that MLLMs often rely too heavily on language priors, leading to inaccurate responses when visual information contradicts these priors or when fine-grained visual understanding is crucial. The authors propose a novel methodology centered on enhancing the model's internal visual representation. Their key contributions include: 1) a new loss function (VISUALLOSS) to enrich the LLM's understanding of images independent of the text, 2) a technique using BLANKTOKENS to reduce the LLM's reliance on language priors, forcing it to attend more to visual cues, and 3) the creation of a targeted synthetic dataset designed to encourage sensitivity to fine-grained visual details. They analyze the impact of their methods through upstream analyses and evaluations on the SpatialEval benchmark, showing improved performance and visually faithful reasoning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**
    *   The paper tackles a well-recognized and important problem in the field of MLLMs: the over-reliance on language priors at the expense of accurate visual understanding.
    *   The proposed techniques, VISUALLOSS and BLANKTOKENS, are well-motivated and clearly explained. VISUALLOSS is particularly compelling as it leverages a rich visual representation (from I-JEPA or other encoders) to directly supervise the LLM's visual tokens.
    *   The use of a synthetic dataset is a good strategy to force the model to focus on visual features, and their design of grid-based spatial reasoning tasks appears effective.
    *   The evaluation is comprehensive, with both upstream analysis to assess visual token prediction and downstream evaluations on SpatialEval, which is appropriate for the task.
    *   The paper's exploration of future extensions (separate modality weights, different image encoders) demonstrate understanding of their method's potential and limitations.
    *   The investigation into how the MLLMs attempt to understand visual information is commendable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   While the results on SpatialEval show improvement, the gains from VISUALLOSS alone are relatively modest, suggesting that it primarily serves as a foundation for the blanking and synthetic data strategies.
    *   The specific details of the implementation, like the architecture for the image encoder and how the blank tokens are implemented, should have been moved into the main paper body.
    *   The evaluation mainly concentrates on spatial reasoning. The results for generalization across diverse tasks are needed.
    *   The claim of a >10pt boost is a bit misleading, as it appears only to be true for a limited test set. More emphasis is needed on tasks with high practical significance.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**
    *   The approach of using VISUALLOSS to strengthen the LLM's internal visual representation is quite novel. The use of I-JEPA as an auxiliary encoder is a clever way to provide more direct visual supervision.
    *   BLANKTOKENS is also an interesting technique to address the language prior problem, although it's conceptually simpler than VISUALLOSS.
    *   The synthetic dataset, tailored to spatial reasoning and visual attribute identification, adds to the value of the work.
    *   The combination of these three techniques creates a valuable contribution to the MLLM research area, improving the ability of the model to focus on specific spatial relationships and visual attribute identification.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   This paper has the potential to influence the design of future MLLM training strategies by highlighting the importance of enriching internal visual representations and reducing reliance on language priors.
    *   The VISUALLOSS and BLANKTOKENS techniques could be adopted or adapted by other researchers in the field.
    *   The synthetic data generation approach could inspire the creation of other targeted datasets to improve specific aspects of MLLM performance.

**Overall:**

The paper provides a good solution to address the important problem of weak visual grounding in MLLMs. The method is innovative, well explained, and validated through experiments. This study will push forward the development of more visually-grounded MLLMs.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Privacy-Preserving Transformers: SwiftKey's Differential Privacy Implementation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05648v1)
- **Authors**: Abdelrahman Abouelenin, Mohamed Abdelrehim, Raffy Fahim, Amr Hendy, Mohamed Afify
- **Abstract**: In this paper we train a transformer using differential privacy (DP) for language modeling in SwiftKey. We run multiple experiments to balance the trade-off between the model size, run-time speed and accuracy. We show that we get small and consistent gains in the next-word-prediction and accuracy with graceful increase in memory and speed compared to the production GRU. This is obtained by scaling down a GPT2 architecture to fit the required size and a two stage training process that builds a seed model on general data and DP finetunes it on typing data. The transformer is integrated using ONNX offering both flexibility and efficiency.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper focuses on training transformer language models using differential privacy (DP) for the SwiftKey keyboard. The key challenge is to balance model size, runtime speed, and accuracy while preserving user privacy during the training process. They achieve this by scaling down a GPT2 architecture, using a two-stage training process: pre-training on general data, followed by DP fine-tuning on anonymized typing data. They integrate the transformer into the SwiftKey system using ONNX for flexibility and efficiency, and demonstrate competitive performance compared to existing GRU models, with only a slightly larger model size.  The authors also explore various techniques to improve performance while maintaining privacy, including hyperparameter tuning for batch size and learning rate, relaxed author sampling, and gradient clipping.  Finally, they experimented with relative positional encodings.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** While the individual components (DP training, transformer architectures, ONNX integration) aren't inherently novel *per se*, the **combination** of these techniques specifically for the resource-constrained, privacy-sensitive environment of a mobile keyboard is notable. The application of DP to transformer-based language models in this specific context appears to be the paper's primary contribution. It is not a novel approach but an application to a specific domain.

*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical and relevant problem: how to improve typing accuracy and prediction quality on mobile devices while respecting user privacy. Successful implementation would have direct impact on the user experience and could influence similar applications in other privacy-sensitive domains.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Focus:** The paper is heavily oriented toward a real-world application (SwiftKey). The experiments are designed to directly address the challenges of deploying DP-trained transformers on mobile devices.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The authors evaluate their models on a variety of metrics (accuracy, NWP, edit rate) and different datasets, providing a relatively thorough picture of their performance.
    *   **Detailed Experimental Setup:** The paper provides considerable details regarding the training process, architecture, and hyperparameters.
    *   **ONNX Integration:** The use of ONNX highlights the practical deployability of the solution.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Improvements:** The gains demonstrated over the production GRU model appear to be modest.  While the authors achieve comparable or slightly better performance, it's not a dramatic leap forward. More concretely, there are relatively small differences between DP and non DP models
    *   **Limited Theoretical Depth:** The paper primarily focuses on empirical results, with relatively little theoretical analysis of the privacy guarantees or the impact of different DP parameters. A deeper discussion of the choice of epsilon and delta would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Limited novelty of approach**: The approach is very straightforward, and doesn't seem to have much ingenuity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could serve as a useful case study for other developers and researchers working on privacy-preserving machine learning for mobile applications. The practical insights and lessons learned could be valuable for others attempting to deploy similar models. It provides a fairly clear recipe for training DP models in resource-constrained environments.

**Overall:** The paper presents a solid engineering effort with a clear application. While the individual techniques aren't groundbreaking, their combined application to the SwiftKey problem is valuable. The primary value lies in demonstrating the feasibility of DP-trained transformers for on-device language modeling. The impact of DP is clear as the performance loss to improve privacy is shown.

**Score: 6**

**Rationale:** A score of 6 reflects the paper's practical significance and comprehensive experimentation, offset by the somewhat incremental improvements and limited theoretical depth. The value lies in the real-world application and the useful insights for others working in the area, rather than groundbreaking innovations.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Unsupervised Blind Speech Separation with a Diffusion Prior
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05657v1)
- **Authors**: Zhongweiyang Xu, Xulin Fan, Zhong-Qiu Wang, Xilin Jiang, Romit Roy Choudhury
- **Abstract**: Blind Speech Separation (BSS) aims to separate multiple speech sources from audio mixtures recorded by a microphone array. The problem is challenging because it is a blind inverse problem, i.e., the microphone array geometry, the room impulse response (RIR), and the speech sources, are all unknown. We propose ArrayDPS to solve the BSS problem in an unsupervised, array-agnostic, and generative manner. The core idea builds on diffusion posterior sampling (DPS), but unlike DPS where the likelihood is tractable, ArrayDPS must approximate the likelihood by formulating a separate optimization problem. The solution to the optimization approximates room acoustics and the relative transfer functions between microphones. These approximations, along with the diffusion priors, iterate through the ArrayDPS sampling process and ultimately yield separated voice sources. We only need a simple single-speaker speech diffusion model as a prior along with the mixtures recorded at the microphones; no microphone array information is necessary. Evaluation results show that ArrayDPS outperforms all baseline unsupervised methods while being comparable to supervised methods in terms of SDR. Audio demos are provided at: https://arraydps.github.io/ArrayDPSDemo/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces ArrayDPS, a novel, unsupervised method for blind speech separation (BSS) using microphone arrays.  Unlike supervised approaches that rely on synthetically generated data and fixed array geometries, ArrayDPS is array-agnostic and operates without needing knowledge of the array configuration, room impulse responses (RIRs), or specific speaker locations.  It leverages diffusion posterior sampling (DPS) but addresses the intractability of the likelihood function in the BSS setting by approximating it through a separate optimization problem that estimates room acoustics and relative transfer functions. The core idea is to use an iterative process combining a speech diffusion prior with estimated room acoustics for source separation. Experiments demonstrate that ArrayDPS outperforms unsupervised baselines and achieves comparable performance to supervised methods on ad-hoc microphone arrays in terms of SDR.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in adapting the DPS framework to the challenging task of unsupervised BSS with microphone arrays, where the likelihood function is intractable due to unknown array geometry and RIRs.  While DPS itself isn't new, its application in this context, combined with the method for approximating the likelihood using Forward Convolutive Prediction (FCP) and Independent Vector Analysis (IVA), is a contribution. It is also novel that it offers a generative approach to solving multi-channel inverse problems using DPS. The approach also solves a key limitation of diffusion-based methods, that are commonly found in image applications, where likelihood is easier to compute, but in audio processing poses challenges.
*   **Significance:** Addressing the limitations of supervised BSS – generalizability issues, deterministic outputs, and fixed array geometries – makes ArrayDPS potentially significant. The unsupervised nature eliminates the need for synthetic training data, reducing the domain mismatch. Being array-agnostic allows for flexibility in real-world applications. The generative approach could potentially offer more nuanced separation results compared to deterministic models.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Addresses a significant challenge in BSS.
    *   Array-agnosticism is a key advantage for real-world applicability.
    *   Generative approach allows sampling of multiple plausible source separations.
    *   Demonstrated improvement over existing unsupervised methods.
    * The paper is well-written and clearly explains the method and its advantages.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The performance while being competitive to supervised methods, is in the order of only achieving similar performance in terms of SDR, still lags behind the supervised approach.
    *   The reliance on approximating the likelihood function introduces a potential bottleneck. The optimization for room acoustics needs to be robust.
    *   The complexity of the method (with DPS, FCP, IVA and a number of hyperparameters) may limit practical adoption.
    *   The experimental evaluation, while showing improvement over unsupervised methods, focuses on limited datasets.  More extensive testing in diverse acoustic environments and array configurations would strengthen the findings.
*   **Impact:**  If the method proves robust and scalable, it could influence the design of more flexible and adaptive speech separation systems. The adoption of unsupervised learning in this domain reduces the need for large, carefully labeled datasets for training. This could also be applied to applications beyond source separation.

**Justification for the Score:**

ArrayDPS presents a valuable contribution to the field of BSS by offering an unsupervised and array-agnostic solution. While the individual components (DPS, IVA, FCP) are established, their integration into a functional framework for unsupervised multi-channel speech separation is novel and the results show a practical unsupervised system that can rival the capabilities of previous supervised methods.  The method offers a promising direction for overcoming the limitations of supervised approaches. However, the complexity and reliance on approximations prevents a very high score. For a more rigorous analysis further ablation studies are needed to identify which components of the methods are most valuable and future research is needed to investigate how to streamline some of the components or approximate them with other more efficient neural network based alternatives.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: eess.AS
- **Score**: 7/10

### Not Like Us, Hunty: Measuring Perceptions and Behavioral Effects of Minoritized Anthropomorphic Cues in LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05660v1)
- **Authors**: Jeffrey Basoah, Daniel Chechelnitsky, Tao Long, Katharina Reinecke, Chrysoula Zerva, Kaitlyn Zhou, Mark Díaz, Maarten Sap
- **Abstract**: As large language models (LLMs) increasingly adapt and personalize to diverse sets of users, there is an increased risk of systems appropriating sociolects, i.e., language styles or dialects that are associated with specific minoritized lived experiences (e.g., African American English, Queer slang). In this work, we examine whether sociolect usage by an LLM agent affects user reliance on its outputs and user perception (satisfaction, frustration, trust, and social presence). We designed and conducted user studies where 498 African American English (AAE) speakers and 487 Queer slang speakers performed a set of question-answering tasks with LLM-based suggestions in either standard American English (SAE) or their self-identified sociolect. Our findings showed that sociolect usage by LLMs influenced both reliance and perceptions, though in some surprising ways. Results suggest that both AAE and Queer slang speakers relied more on the SAE agent, and had more positive perceptions of the SAE agent. Yet, only Queer slang speakers felt more social presence from the Queer slang agent over the SAE one, whereas only AAE speakers preferred and trusted the SAE agent over the AAE one. These findings emphasize the need to test for behavioral outcomes rather than simply assume that personalization would lead to a better and safer reliance outcome. They also highlight the nuanced dynamics of minoritized language in machine interactions, underscoring the need for LLMs to be carefully designed to respect cultural and linguistic boundaries while fostering genuine user engagement and trust.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the impact of using minoritized sociolects (African American English (AAE) and Queer slang) in Large Language Models (LLMs) on user reliance and perception.  Two within-subjects studies were conducted, one with AAE speakers and another with Queer slang speakers, where participants interacted with LLM-based suggestions in either Standard American English (SAE) or their identified sociolect.  The results revealed that participants generally preferred and relied more on the SAE LLM, although Queer slang speakers reported higher social presence from the LLM using Queer slang. Qualitative analysis of rationales highlighted the nuances in user perceptions, showing a complex relationship between language, behavior, and cultural sensitivity in machine interactions. The authors argue for careful design considerations in LLMs to respect cultural and linguistic boundaries and to avoid cultural appropriation, emphasizing the importance of measuring behavioral outcomes alongside user perceptions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty and Significance:** The paper addresses a timely and important issue: the ethical and practical implications of personalizing LLMs with minoritized language. The study's focus on both AAE and Queer slang is valuable given the historical biases against these linguistic groups in AI systems. The paper’s use of behavioral metrics, not just self-reported perceptions, is a significant strength. It challenges assumptions about personalization automatically leading to better outcomes.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The within-subjects design, large sample sizes, careful prompt design, and counterbalancing are methodologically sound.
    *   **Mixed-Methods Approach:** The combination of quantitative analysis (reliance scores, perception scales) with qualitative analysis (thematic coding of user rationales) provides a richer and more nuanced understanding of the phenomena.
    *   **Counter-Intuitive Findings:** The finding that users often *prefer* the SAE LLM over one using their own sociolect is surprising and thought-provoking, challenging the simple assumption that personalization is always beneficial.
    *   **Practical Implications:** The paper's recommendations for context-aware language adaptation and user-centered feedback in LLM design are practical and valuable.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Simulated LLM:** The use of pre-generated responses, while allowing for controlled experimentation, limits the ecological validity of the study. It's not a true interactive LLM.
    *   **Limited Sociolinguistic Nuance:** The paper acknowledges that sociolect use varies geographically and demographically, and that their study has limited scope as a result.
    *   **Generalizability:** The focus on two specific sociolects means the findings may not be directly generalizable to all other minoritized languages.
    *   **Intersectionality:**  The study acknowledges that lack of data for participants identifying with both AAE and Queer slang, limiting insights for intersectional use cases.

*   **Impact:**
    *   The paper makes a significant contribution to the growing body of work on fairness, accountability, and transparency in LLMs.
    *   It highlights the need for a more nuanced understanding of linguistic bias and cultural sensitivity in AI design.
    *   It encourages researchers to move beyond simply measuring user perceptions and to consider behavioral outcomes when evaluating personalization strategies.
    *   The findings could inform guidelines and best practices for developing LLMs that are more respectful and inclusive.

*Rationale for Assigned Score:*

While the paper makes valuable contributions to understanding the nuances of sociolect usage in LLMs, certain limitations temper its impact.  The simulated LLM environment and acknowledged scope limitations on geographic and population variance of linguistic groups prevent the study from reaching its full potential for ecological validity and generalizability. A higher score would require evidence in the study of the benefits in terms of user perception and engagement.

### Adaptive Stress Testing Black-Box LLM Planners
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05665v1)
- **Authors**: Neeloy Chakraborty, John Pohovey, Melkior Ornik, Katherine Driggs-Campbell
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have recently demonstrated success in generalizing across decision-making tasks including planning, control and prediction, but their tendency to hallucinate unsafe and undesired outputs poses risks. We argue that detecting such failures is necessary, especially in safety-critical scenarios. Existing black-box methods often detect hallucinations by identifying inconsistencies across multiple samples. Many of these approaches typically introduce prompt perturbations like randomizing detail order or generating adversarial inputs, with the intuition that a confident model should produce stable outputs. We first perform a manual case study showing that other forms of perturbations (e.g., adding noise, removing sensor details) cause LLMs to hallucinate in a driving environment. We then propose a novel method for efficiently searching the space of prompt perturbations using Adaptive Stress Testing (AST) with Monte-Carlo Tree Search (MCTS). Our AST formulation enables discovery of scenarios and prompts that cause language models to act with high uncertainty. By generating MCTS prompt perturbation trees across diverse scenarios, we show that offline analyses can be used at runtime to automatically generate prompts that influence model uncertainty, and to inform real-time trust assessments of an LLM.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Adaptive Stress Testing Black-Box LLM Planners":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the problem of hallucination and unreliable behavior in Large Language Models (LLMs) used as planners, particularly in safety-critical scenarios like autonomous driving. The authors propose a black-box approach using Adaptive Stress Testing (AST) with Monte Carlo Tree Search (MCTS) to systematically perturb prompts and identify scenarios that induce uncertainty in the LLM's decision-making. The core idea is to characterize the LLM's robustness to variations in input observations, without relying on internal model parameters or token probabilities. The framework aims to automatically find and exploit weaknesses in the LLM's planning capabilities, generating prompts that either increase inconsistent outputs or minimize contradictions, and identifying situations where the LLM is uncertain. The paper presents a manual case study highlighting the hallucination tendency of LLMs in a driving environment, followed by a demonstration of the AST-MCTS approach.  The authors argue that these stress tests can be used at runtime to inform real-time trust assessments of the LLM.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in the application of AST with MCTS to the specific problem of characterizing and addressing hallucinations in black-box LLM planners. While AST and MCTS are not new concepts, their combination in this context and the focus on generating prompts grounded in the observation space of the environment offer a novel perspective. The approach avoids the common pitfalls of feeding LLMs obviously incorrect or nonsensical information. It's a systematic way to *probe* the LLM's understanding rather than just tricking it.

*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is highly significant.  As LLMs become more prevalent in safety-critical systems, ensuring their reliability and detecting failure modes is paramount. The ability to automatically identify scenarios where an LLM becomes uncertain or inconsistent provides a valuable tool for validation and verification. The promise of using these tests at *runtime* is attractive for building trust frameworks in unpredictable conditions. That said, the actual implementation and verification of the real-time aspect is not as fully fleshed out in this paper.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Problem Relevance:** The paper directly addresses a critical challenge in the deployment of LLMs for planning tasks.
    *   **Black-Box Approach:** The method's reliance on black-box techniques makes it applicable to a wide range of LLMs, even those with restricted access.
    *   **Systematic Approach:** AST-MCTS provides a structured way to explore the prompt space and identify vulnerabilities.
    *   **Grounded Prompts:** The generated prompts are based on environment observations, increasing their relevance and interpretability.
    *   **Offline & Runtime Application:**  The study presents a pathway for not only generating tests, but also utilizing data from tests at runtime.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Empirical Validation:** The experiments focus on a specific highway driving environment. While the results are promising, the generalizability of the approach to other planning domains (e.g., robotics, logistics) needs further investigation.
    *   **Complexity of the AST Simulator:** Creating the AST simulator, including defining the prompt perturbation space and reward function, requires domain knowledge and can be non-trivial. This might limit the accessibility and adoption of the method. The paper claims the AST generated prompts are "legible guidance" for prompt design and finetuning but this requires further demonstration/elaboration as they are ultimately "perturbed" versions.
    *   **Reliance on a single diversity measure:** The diversity measure is somewhat rudimentary. Although Shannon Entropy is suggested in a pinch, more sophisticated metrics that consider the *semantics* and *safety* of the generated actions would likely improve the effectiveness of the approach. The current diversity metric favors generating *any* random action, but doesn't discriminate between a *dangerous* action and a relatively harmless one.
    *   **Limited scale of study for downstream impact:** Downstream potential is largely claimed at this point in time with a very small scale study. Further exploration in future works is critical to support these high impact promises.
    *   **Choice of Proxy LLM:** There are notable claims and references to other works utilizing proxy LLMs and in this work, the authors express reservations about this approach. It's important to ensure that if another LLM is performing any kind of proxy assistance or analysis, that the same rigorous stress testing approach is deployed for it to ensure that it isn't also hallucinating.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence research on:

    *   Robustness evaluation of LLMs in planning and decision-making.
    *   Development of trust and safety mechanisms for LLM-based autonomous systems.
    *   Adversarial prompting techniques for uncovering weaknesses in LLMs.
    *   Applications of AST and MCTS to other challenging AI safety problems.

**Overall Score:**

The paper presents a novel and promising approach to a significant problem. While the experimental validation is somewhat limited and the reliance on basic diversity measures needs further refinement, the core ideas are solid and the potential impact is substantial. The methodology has significant limitations, though, and would be insufficient without more complete future works.

### Lost in OCR Translation? Vision-Based Approaches to Robust Document Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05666v1)
- **Authors**: Alexander Most, Joseph Winjum, Ayan Biswas, Shawn Jones, Nishath Rajiv Ranasinghe, Dan O'Malley, Manish Bhattarai
- **Abstract**: Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) has become a popular technique for enhancing the reliability and utility of Large Language Models (LLMs) by grounding responses in external documents. Traditional RAG systems rely on Optical Character Recognition (OCR) to first process scanned documents into text. However, even state-of-the-art OCRs can introduce errors, especially in degraded or complex documents. Recent vision-language approaches, such as ColPali, propose direct visual embedding of documents, eliminating the need for OCR. This study presents a systematic comparison between a vision-based RAG system (ColPali) and more traditional OCR-based pipelines utilizing Llama 3.2 (90B) and Nougat OCR across varying document qualities. Beyond conventional retrieval accuracy metrics, we introduce a semantic answer evaluation benchmark to assess end-to-end question-answering performance. Our findings indicate that while vision-based RAG performs well on documents it has been fine-tuned on, OCR-based RAG is better able to generalize to unseen documents of varying quality. We highlight the key trade-offs between computational efficiency and semantic accuracy, offering practical guidance for RAG practitioners in selecting between OCR-dependent and vision-based document retrieval systems in production environments.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the performance of vision-based Retrieval-Augmented Generation (RAG) versus OCR-based RAG pipelines for document retrieval under varying document quality conditions. The authors compare ColQwen2 (a vision-language model) against Llama 3.2 (90B) with Nougat OCR, and Llama 3.2 with the Qwen embedding model.  They introduce a new dataset, DocDeg, that features various levels of visual degradation. Beyond traditional retrieval metrics, the authors also introduce a semantic answer evaluation benchmark. The results suggest that OCR-based RAG, particularly when employing high-quality OCR like Llama 3.2, generalizes better to unseen documents, while VLM-based RAG excels in computational efficiency. They ultimately suggest trading computational efficiency over semantic accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**

    *   The paper offers a valuable comparative analysis of VLM-based RAG (specifically ColPali) and a modern OCR-based RAG pipeline.  The explicit focus on comparing against a highly capable OCR system like Llama 3.2 is a key strength, as many previous studies relied on outdated baselines.
    *   The creation of the DocDeg dataset addresses a gap in existing benchmarks, which often focus on clean documents.  The annotation of degradation levels is a meaningful addition.
    *   The introduction of a semantic answer evaluation benchmark adds to the rigor of the study, moving beyond simple retrieval accuracy to assess the downstream performance. This is a major strength.
    *   While the core concept of comparing OCR and VLM-based approaches isn't entirely novel, the comprehensive experimental design, the new dataset, and semantic evaluation do represent a significant advancement.

*   **Significance:**

    *   The paper's findings provide practical guidance for RAG practitioners, highlighting the trade-offs between computational efficiency and semantic accuracy. This guidance is particularly relevant in production environments where document quality varies widely.
    *   The demonstration that OCR-based RAG (with good OCR) can outperform VLM-based RAG in terms of semantic accuracy, even with larger models is significant. It challenges the assumption that VLMs are inherently more robust to visual noise.
    *   The study's analysis of computational efficiency, including embedding time, retrieval latency, and memory usage, is also highly valuable for practical deployment considerations.
    *   The focus on models readily deployable and comparable is significant in the sense that this paper addresses a realistic problem and offers realistic comparisons that are useful to RAG practitioners.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive experimental design with careful control over document quality levels.
    *   Use of a new, annotated dataset that addresses a critical gap in existing benchmarks.
    *   Introduction of a semantic answer evaluation benchmark that provides a more holistic assessment of RAG performance.
    *   Explicit consideration of computational efficiency, which is crucial for practical deployment.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   While the paper uses Llama 3.2 (90B) for OCR, it uses ColQwen2 (7B) which is a significantly smaller VLM to embed images. The authors explain that using Llama 3.2 to create patch embeddings and perform retrieval would create significant memory and latency issues. However, there is not enough explanation around this.
    *   The explanation around the need to add OCR to images retrieved by ColPali to make the evaluation possible is not robust enough.
    *   The qualitative analysis in the experiment is limited to a short statement around the question-answer pairs being of good quality. Further investigation could be done to confirm the results of the evaluation.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   This work could influence future research by encouraging the development of more robust evaluation methodologies for RAG systems, particularly those that consider document quality and semantic accuracy.
    *   The DocDeg dataset could become a valuable resource for the community, facilitating further research on document retrieval under realistic conditions.
    *   The paper's practical guidance could inform the design and deployment of RAG systems in production environments, leading to improved performance and usability.

**Overall:**

The paper provides a solid contribution to the field of document retrieval and RAG by providing a comparative analysis between a VLM-based approach and an OCR-based approach. The authors present strong data to confirm their findings. Overall, this paper presents a good argument for choosing OCR-based RAG with a high-quality OCR system in noisy or varying quality data.

### InstanceGen: Image Generation with Instance-level Instructions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05678v1)
- **Authors**: Etai Sella, Yanir Kleiman, Hadar Averbuch-Elor
- **Abstract**: Despite rapid advancements in the capabilities of generative models, pretrained text-to-image models still struggle in capturing the semantics conveyed by complex prompts that compound multiple objects and instance-level attributes. Consequently, we are witnessing growing interests in integrating additional structural constraints, %leveraging additional structural inputs typically in the form of coarse bounding boxes, to better guide the generation process in such challenging cases. In this work, we take the idea of structural guidance a step further by making the observation that contemporary image generation models can directly provide a plausible \emph{fine-grained} structural initialization. We propose a technique that couples this image-based structural guidance with LLM-based instance-level instructions, yielding output images that adhere to all parts of the text prompt, including object counts, instance-level attributes, and spatial relations between instances.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the InstanceGen paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "InstanceGen: Image Generation with Instance-level Instructions" addresses the challenge of generating images from complex text prompts that specify multiple objects, their individual attributes, and their spatial relationships. The method combines image-based structural guidance from a pre-trained text-to-image model with instance-level instructions extracted using a Large Language Model (LLM).  The process involves: 1) generating an initial image, 2) segmenting the image into instances using attention maps and segmentation models, 3) using an LLM to assign object identities and attributes to each segment, and 4) refining the image using attention-based losses to enforce these assignments while maintaining visual quality. They also introduce a new benchmark, CompoundPrompts, to evaluate these capabilities, which is broken down into three levels of difficulty based on object counts, instance-level attributes, and spatial relations. The method is demonstrated to achieve state-of-the-art results, particularly on complex prompts.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *integration* of existing techniques (diffusion models, segmentation, LLMs) in a specific way to tackle a well-defined problem. While each component is not individually novel, the way they are combined to achieve instance-level control is a unique contribution. The idea of using the initial image generation not just as a starting point but to inform the *structure* of the layout before LLM assignment is clever. It leverages the inherent structural understanding of diffusion models. Also the self-correction mechanism in the assigning stage and a careful balance between attention guidance, and structure perseveration make it stand out.
*   **Significance:** The problem addressed is undeniably important. The ability to generate images that precisely adhere to complex, multi-object prompts is a critical step toward more controllable and useful image generation systems. This paper's success on complex prompt fidelity opens doors to more advanced applications requiring fine-grained image control. The CompoundPrompts dataset is also a valuable contribution, filling a gap in existing benchmarks for multi-object attribute control.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   Strong empirical results: The paper demonstrates significant improvements over existing methods on both the DrawBench and CompoundPrompts datasets. Qualitative results are also convincing.
    *   Well-defined approach: The steps are clearly outlined, and the rationale for each component is well-explained.
    *   Addresses a clear limitation: It tackles a specific failure mode of text-to-image models, which is precisely rendering complex multi-object scenes.
    *   Ablation study provides insight: It clearly showcases the contribution of each component in the model.
    *   The prompt engineering to make LLM produce assignments and the careful design for image generation are two key features of the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Dependency on LLM: The reliance on the LLM for instance assignment means the system's performance is directly tied to the capabilities and biases of the LLM. Errors in the LLM's reasoning propagate to the final image. Also, the cost of relying on LLM for each forward pass is high and might be a deterrent for large-scale use.
    *   Complexity: The method is relatively complex, involving several stages and components. This complexity can make it difficult to implement and optimize.
    *   Scalability: Although the paper is tested across many scenarios and datasets, it still lacks proof of generality. Also as prompts grow, this system might have challenges due to relying on LLM.
    *   Limited evaluation metrics: While VQA metrics capture some aspects of the prompt fidelity, it’s not a perfect measure. More human evaluations would strengthen the conclusions.
    *   Failure cases: The paper acknowledges limitations regarding depth perception in object placement (i.e. front vs. back) and certain limitations regarding combining different objects together for spatial layouts, and more complex counting of objects.
*   **Potential Influence:** The paper is likely to influence future research in controllable image generation.  The idea of leveraging an initial image generation's structure to inform layout is a promising direction. The CompoundPrompts dataset will serve as a valuable tool for evaluating and comparing future methods.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and effective approach to a significant problem in text-to-image generation. It provides strong empirical results and a valuable new benchmark. While it relies on existing techniques and faces certain limitations, the creative integration of these techniques and the performance gains warrant a high score. This method addresses a key challenge, showcasing how a combination of structural guidance from diffusion models and instruction following from LLMs can improve prompt fidelity in complex scenarios.

### From Bias To Improved Prompts: A Case Study of Bias Mitigation of Clone Detection Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05679v1)
- **Authors**: QiHong Chen, Lianghao Jiang, Iftekhar Ahmed
- **Abstract**: The issue of clone code has persisted in software engineering, primarily because developers often copy and paste code segments. This common practice has elevated the importance of clone code detection, garnering attention from both software engineering researchers and industry professionals. Their collective concern arises from the potential negative impacts that clone code can have on software quality. The emergence of powerful Generative Large Language Models (LLMs) like ChatGPT has exacerbated the clone code problem. These advanced models possess code generation capabilities that can inadvertently create code clones. As a result, the need to detect clone code has become more critical than ever before. In this study, we assess the suitability of LLMs for clone code detection. Our results demonstrate that the Palm model achieved a high F1 score of 89.30 for the avatar dataset and 86.41 for the poolC dataset. A known issue with LLMs is their susceptibility to prompt bias, where the performance of these models fluctuates based on the input prompt provided. In our research, we delve deeper into the reasons behind these fluctuations and propose a framework to mitigate prompt bias for clone detection. Our analysis identifies eight distinct categories of prompt bias, and our devised approach leveraging these biases yields a significant improvement of up to 10.81% in the F1 score. These findings underscore the substantial impact of prompt bias on the performance of LLMs and highlight the potential for leveraging model errors to alleviate this bias.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

This paper investigates the use of large language models (LLMs) for clone code detection. The authors find that LLMs like PaLM perform well on this task, achieving high F1 scores. However, they also highlight the susceptibility of LLMs to prompt bias, where small changes in the input prompt can significantly affect performance.  The paper then delves into the reasons behind these fluctuations, identifies eight categories of prompt bias related to LLMs' misunderstanding of the clone code task, and proposes a framework to mitigate this bias by incorporating "prompt bias lessons." Their approach involves analyzing model errors, categorizing them, and adding lessons derived from these categories to improve prompt effectiveness. Experiments show that using these "prompt bias lessons" leads to a significant improvement in F1 scores, underscoring the impact and potential for leveraging model errors.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper offers a valuable contribution to the growing body of research on using LLMs in software engineering. Its novelty lies in several aspects:

*   **LLMs for Clone Detection:** While LLMs are being used in many SE tasks, clone detection hasn't been widely explored, and the paper bridges this gap. This alone is worth noting.
*   **Focus on Prompt Bias from an Error Perspective:** Rather than simply searching for better prompts, the authors systematically investigate *why* the models struggle, analyzing errors, and categorizing them.  This is a significant shift in understanding prompt bias.
*   **Bias Mitigation Framework:** The approach of crafting "prompt bias lessons" based on error categories is innovative. It provides a structured way to improve model performance by addressing underlying misunderstandings.

**Strengths:**

*   **Rigorous Methodology:** The paper uses clearly defined experimental setups, including model selection, datasets, and evaluation metrics. The ablation study helps to assess the impact of individual prompt bias lessons. The use of Cohen's kappa and statistical significance tests adds rigor.
*   **Identified Bias Categories:** The identified prompt bias categories are insightful and well-defined. They provide a useful taxonomy for understanding how LLMs can misinterpret clone detection tasks.
*   **Quantifiable Improvements:** The paper demonstrates significant performance improvements through the proposed bias mitigation framework. The empirical evidence is compelling.
*   **Clear Writing and Organization:** The paper is well-structured and easy to follow.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Manual Error Analysis:** The process of identifying prompt bias categories and crafting lessons relies on manual analysis. This is labor-intensive and potentially subjective. Automating this process would be a valuable extension. While the high Cohen's kappa minimizes the risk of bias, it is not eliminated.
*   **Limited Scope of Datasets:** While the chosen datasets (PoolC and a constructed cross-language dataset) are appropriate, the findings may not generalize to all clone detection scenarios or programming languages.
*   **Generalizability of Prompt Bias Categories:** While the "prompt bias lessons" had success in this study, the categories identified are not necessarily exhaustive.

**Significance:**

The findings have practical implications for developers using LLMs in software engineering tasks like clone detection. The proposed framework provides a way to improve model accuracy by addressing prompt bias. It pushes beyond 'black box' use of LLMs by encouraging analyzing and correcting misunderstandings.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper is not revolutionary, but its rigorous methodology, novel approach to prompt bias, and quantifiable improvements justify a high score. The biggest weakness is the manual nature of the bias identification, which limits scalability and introduces potential subjectivity. The practical relevance is clear.

### Fine-Tuning Video-Text Contrastive Model for Primate Behavior Retrieval from Unlabeled Raw Videos
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05681v1)
- **Authors**: Giulio Cesare Mastrocinque Santo, Patrícia Izar, Irene Delval, Victor de Napole Gregolin, Nina S. T. Hirata
- **Abstract**: Video recordings of nonhuman primates in their natural habitat are a common source for studying their behavior in the wild. We fine-tune pre-trained video-text foundational models for the specific domain of capuchin monkeys, with the goal of developing useful computational models to help researchers to retrieve useful clips from videos. We focus on the challenging problem of training a model based solely on raw, unlabeled video footage, using weak audio descriptions sometimes provided by field collaborators. We leverage recent advances in Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) and Vision-Language Models (VLMs) to address the extremely noisy nature of both video and audio content. Specifically, we propose a two-folded approach: an agentic data treatment pipeline and a fine-tuning process. The data processing pipeline automatically extracts clean and semantically aligned video-text pairs from the raw videos, which are subsequently used to fine-tune a pre-trained Microsoft's X-CLIP model through Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA). We obtained an uplift in $Hits@5$ of $167\%$ for the 16 frames model and an uplift of $114\%$ for the 8 frame model on our domain data. Moreover, based on $NDCG@K$ results, our model is able to rank well most of the considered behaviors, while the tested raw pre-trained models are not able to rank them at all. The code will be made available upon acceptance.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of retrieving relevant video clips of capuchin monkey behavior from a large, unlabeled dataset of raw video footage. The authors fine-tune a pre-trained video-text contrastive model (Microsoft's X-CLIP) specifically for this domain using solely raw video with weak audio descriptions. Their approach involves a two-pronged strategy: (1) an agentic data treatment pipeline that leverages Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) like Whisper, BLIP-2, and LLaMA to automatically extract clean and semantically aligned video-text pairs, and (2) Low-Rank Adaptation (LoRA) to efficiently fine-tune the X-CLIP model using these extracted pairs. The authors demonstrate substantial performance improvements in video-text retrieval and zero-shot classification tasks compared to raw pre-trained X-CLIP models. They show that their approach can effectively process noisy video and audio content.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Addresses a practical and important problem:** Studying animal behavior in the wild often relies on the manual review of vast amounts of noisy video data. Automating this process has significant practical value for researchers.
    *   **Effective use of MLLMs and LoRA:** The paper demonstrates the effective application of recent advances in MLLMs and parameter-efficient fine-tuning to a specific domain. The data treatment pipeline is a clever way to leverage weak audio descriptions to create training data where manual annotation would be too costly.
    *   **Significant performance improvements:** The quantitative results show substantial improvements in retrieval accuracy (Hits@K) and ranking ability (NDCG@K) compared to the baseline pre-trained models. The 167% and 114% improvement in Hits@5 is notable.
    *   **Qualitative examples illustrate generalization:** The retrieved video clips based on textual descriptions such as a "young monkey nursing" and a "monkey swinging on a vine" further prove the ability of the fine-tuned model to capture meaningful behaviors.
    *   **Clear and well-structured presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly explains the methodology and experimental setup.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on a limited dataset:** While the authors state that they plan to expand their datasets in the future, the current amount of domain-specific data is limited. This is a common issue in fine-tuning projects. The effectiveness in larger datasets could also change.
    *   **Limited exploration of the Ethogram.** The table shows the different actions and descriptions that the tool uses to filter the transcripts. Further exploration and testing for the effects that each of the actions have during the filtering process are not performed. The paper would benefit from testing if the filter could work using other actions with different definitions.
    *   **Lack of comparison to other fine-tuning methods.** While the paper mentions Prompt Tuning and Adapter Tuning in the related works section, the paper's novelty would be stronger if they compare their fine-tuned model with them.
    *   **Zero-shot classification improvements are lower.** Zero-shot classification tasks had lower improvements. This brings into question whether the video encoder is able to correctly classify unseen videos. This may be partially due to a reliance on the specific dataset that had a limited variety of videos.

*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The use of MLLM-based agents for cleaning and aligning noisy video-text data in this specific domain (capuchin monkey behavior) appears novel. While MLLMs are increasingly used in various applications, this particular application is non-trivial.
    *   The successful application of LoRA to fine-tune a video-text model in this domain is a valuable contribution. While LoRA is gaining popularity, its application to vision models is not as widespread as in LLMs.
    *   The paper provides a practical framework for building video retrieval systems from unlabeled data, which can be beneficial in other domains where manual annotation is infeasible.

*   **Potential Influence:**

    *   The paper could encourage further research into using MLLMs and parameter-efficient fine-tuning techniques for processing and analyzing large, unlabeled video datasets in other fields (e.g., other animal behavior studies, environmental monitoring, medical imaging).
    *   The data treatment pipeline could serve as a template for other researchers facing similar challenges with noisy data.

*   **Critical Score Rationale:**

    The paper presents a solid contribution to the field of video retrieval and animal behavior analysis. The practical problem it addresses, the clever use of MLLMs and LoRA, and the significant performance improvements justify a favorable score. The weaknesses discussed, particularly the limited dataset size and exploration of the ethogram, and the reliance on comparing similar models prevent it from receiving a higher mark.

### Assessing Robustness to Spurious Correlations in Post-Training Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05704v1)
- **Authors**: Julia Shuieh, Prasann Singhal, Apaar Shanker, John Heyer, George Pu, Samuel Denton
- **Abstract**: Supervised and preference-based fine-tuning techniques have become popular for aligning large language models (LLMs) with user intent and correctness criteria. However, real-world training data often exhibits spurious correlations -- arising from biases, dataset artifacts, or other "shortcut" features -- that can compromise a model's performance or generalization. In this paper, we systematically evaluate three post-training algorithms -- Supervised Fine-Tuning (SFT), Direct Preference Optimization (DPO), and KTO (Kahneman-Tversky Optimization) -- across a diverse set of synthetic tasks and spuriousness conditions. Our tasks span mathematical reasoning, constrained instruction-following, and document-grounded question answering. We vary the degree of spurious correlation (10% vs. 90%) and investigate two forms of artifacts: "Feature Ambiguity" and "Distributional Narrowness." Our results show that the models often but not always degrade under higher spuriousness. The preference-based methods (DPO/KTO) can demonstrate relative robustness in mathematical reasoning tasks. By contrast, SFT maintains stronger performance in complex, context-intensive tasks. These findings highlight that no single post-training strategy universally outperforms in all scenarios; the best choice depends on the type of target task and the nature of spurious correlations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper investigates the robustness of post-training language model alignment techniques (SFT, DPO, KTO) to spurious correlations in training data.  The authors create synthetic datasets across three tasks (DocQA, Math Reasoning, Constrained Instruction Following) with varying levels (10% vs. 90%) of spurious correlations, and two types: "Feature Ambiguity" (FA) and "Distributional Narrowness" (DN). They find that no single alignment strategy is universally superior. Preference-based methods (DPO/KTO) can show relative robustness in mathematical reasoning, while SFT performs better in complex, context-intensive tasks like DocQA. The impact of increased spuriousness is not always detrimental and depends on the task and alignment method.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Systematic Evaluation:** The paper provides a valuable, controlled experimental setup to examine the impact of spurious correlations. The synthetic datasets and controlled injection of spuriousness allow for clear insights.
    *   **Comparative Analysis:** The comparison of SFT, DPO, and KTO across different tasks and spuriousness types provides a nuanced understanding of their strengths and weaknesses. The experiments showing relative improvements vs. degradation with high spuriousness are important.
    *   **Practical Relevance:**  The findings are directly applicable to practitioners aligning LLMs, highlighting the need to choose alignment methods carefully based on the characteristics of the data and the target task.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Synthetic Data:**  While the use of synthetic data provides control, it also limits the generalizability of the findings to real-world scenarios where spurious correlations are more complex and subtle. The degree to which Feature Ambiguity and Distributional Narrowness truly mimic real-world correlations is open for debate.
    *   **Limited Task Coverage:**  Although the tasks are diverse, exploring a wider range of tasks and spurious correlation types would further strengthen the conclusions.
    *   **Interpretability:** While the paper observes performance variations, the precise mechanisms by which spurious correlations influence different alignment methods aren't fully elucidated. Further analysis to identify the learned spurious features may add substantial insight.
    *   **LLM-as-Judge in DocQA:** While necessary for open-ended tasks, the use of LLM-as-Judge introduces potential biases and variance in the evaluation. Alternative automated evaluation metrics could improve robustness.
*   **Novelty and Significance:**

    *   The paper's novelty stems from its systematic exploration of different alignment methods under controlled spuriousness conditions. It provides valuable empirical evidence that complements existing theoretical understanding of spurious correlations.
    *   The significance lies in its practical recommendations for practitioners selecting alignment methods and mitigating the impact of biases in real-world training data.

*   **Potential Influence on the Field:**

    *   The paper can influence future research by promoting more rigorous evaluations of alignment methods under controlled spuriousness conditions.
    *   It can also guide the development of more robust alignment algorithms that are less susceptible to spurious correlations.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper represents a solid and well-executed empirical study. However, the reliance on synthetic data and somewhat limited task coverage tempers its generalizability. Its strength lies in highlighting the trade-offs between different alignment methods, thereby contributing to a better understanding of alignment robustness.
