### LLM-Text Watermarking based on Lagrange Interpolation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05712v1)
- **Authors**: Jarosław Janas, Paweł Morawiecki, Josef Pieprzyk
- **Abstract**: The rapid advancement of LLMs (Large Language Models) has established them as a foundational technology for many AI and ML powered human computer interactions. A critical challenge in this context is the attribution of LLM-generated text, either to the specific language model used or to the individual user who generated it. This is essential for combating misinformation, fake news, misinterpretation, and plagiarism. One of the key techniques for addressing this issue is watermarking. This work presents a watermarking scheme for LLM-generated text based on Lagrange interpolation, which enables the recovery of a secret author identity even when the text has been heavily redacted by an adversary. The core idea is to embed a continuous sequence of points (x, f(x)) that lie on a single straight line. The x-coordinates are generated pseudorandomly using either an LFSR (when security is not a priority) or a cryptographically secure NFSR for high-security applications. The scheme efficiency and resilience to adversarial modifications are analysed. Experimental results show that the proposed method is highly effective, allowing the recovery of the author identity when as few as three points survive adversarial manipulation.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel watermarking scheme for Large Language Model (LLM)-generated text based on Lagrange interpolation over a finite field. The core idea is to embed points from a straight line *f(x)* into the text, where the x-coordinates are generated pseudorandomly. The secret identity of the author is encoded in the coefficients of the line. To recover the watermark, the verifier needs to solve the Maximum Collinear Points (MCP) problem to identify the line. The authors also propose a more robust version of the scheme using cryptographically secure pseudorandom number generators and dynamic token partitioning to mitigate algebraic attacks.  They analyze the scheme's robustness to adversarial modifications and provide experimental results. The paper also explores extensions to multiple secrets and higher-degree polynomials.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The idea of using Lagrange interpolation for watermarking LLM-generated text appears novel. It provides a unique way to embed information in the text structure, linking token selection to a secret key.  The use of the Maximum Collinear Points problem for watermark extraction is also a creative approach. However, the novelty is somewhat tempered by the fact that the core building blocks (Lagrange interpolation, MCP) are well-established techniques. The application to LLMs and the specific adaptations for text watermarking are where the novelty lies.

*   **Significance:**  The problem of attributing LLM-generated text is a very important one, particularly with increasing concerns around misinformation and plagiarism. While the paper's scheme addresses this problem, its practical significance depends on its robustness and efficiency. The algebraic attacks they address are relevant, but the performance overhead introduced by the countermeasures (NFSR, dynamic partitioning) could limit its applicability.  The probabilistic analysis offers insight into the scheme's resilience, but more extensive real-world testing would be required to fully assess its significance. The extensions to multiple secrets and polynomials, while interesting, are not as fully fleshed out and their practical value needs more analysis. Furthermore, a thorough comparison to state-of-the-art watermarking schemes for LLMs regarding metrics like imperceptibility, robustness, and false positive rate is lacking.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem definition and well-articulated motivations.
    *   A novel approach to watermarking using Lagrange interpolation.
    *   Addresses and mitigates potential algebraic attacks.
    *   Provides a probabilistic analysis of the scheme's robustness.
    *   Explores extensions to multiple secrets and higher-degree polynomials.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Lack of comparison with existing state-of-the-art watermarking schemes on standard benchmark datasets.
    *   The performance overhead of countermeasures (NFSR, dynamic partitioning) could limit practicality.
    *   Limited experimental evaluation, especially in real-world scenarios with varying degrees of adversarial editing.
    *   The discussion on multiple secrets and higher-degree polynomials is not as complete as the core scheme.
    *   The assumption that the adversary will target larger sections of the text. More study needs to be done on the impact of token level removal, replacement, or insertion, and the scheme's resilience to these attacks.
    *   Limited study of the imperceptibility aspect, for example, measuring the perplexity increase introduced by the watermarking scheme.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence research in LLM watermarking by introducing a new approach based on algebraic methods.  It could spur further research into exploring other algebraic techniques for watermarking and improving the robustness and efficiency of Lagrange interpolation-based schemes. It also highlights the importance of considering algebraic attacks in watermarking designs.  However, its ultimate influence will depend on how well it performs in real-world scenarios and how it compares to existing techniques.

**Rigorous Rationale for Score:**

The paper presents a genuinely novel watermarking method for LLMs using Lagrange Interpolation, an approach that has not been widely explored previously. The authors address a vital problem and provide solutions to increase its robustness. While the theoretical analysis looks promising, experimental evidence and the lack of discussion about its imperceptibility reduces the overall score.

### Semantic-Space-Intervened Diffusive Alignment for Visual Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05721v1)
- **Authors**: Zixuan Li, Lei Meng, Guoqing Chao, Wei Wu, Xiaoshuo Yan, Yimeng Yang, Zhuang Qi, Xiangxu Meng
- **Abstract**: Cross-modal alignment is an effective approach to improving visual classification. Existing studies typically enforce a one-step mapping that uses deep neural networks to project the visual features to mimic the distribution of textual features. However, they typically face difficulties in finding such a projection due to the two modalities in both the distribution of class-wise samples and the range of their feature values. To address this issue, this paper proposes a novel Semantic-Space-Intervened Diffusive Alignment method, termed SeDA, models a semantic space as a bridge in the visual-to-textual projection, considering both types of features share the same class-level information in classification. More importantly, a bi-stage diffusion framework is developed to enable the progressive alignment between the two modalities. Specifically, SeDA first employs a Diffusion-Controlled Semantic Learner to model the semantic features space of visual features by constraining the interactive features of the diffusion model and the category centers of visual features. In the later stage of SeDA, the Diffusion-Controlled Semantic Translator focuses on learning the distribution of textual features from the semantic space. Meanwhile, the Progressive Feature Interaction Network introduces stepwise feature interactions at each alignment step, progressively integrating textual information into mapped features. Experimental results show that SeDA achieves stronger cross-modal feature alignment, leading to superior performance over existing methods across multiple scenarios.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a novel "Semantic-Space-Intervened Diffusive Alignment" (SeDA) method for cross-modal alignment in visual classification. The core idea is to use a semantic space as a bridge between visual and textual features, aiming to alleviate heterogeneity. SeDA employs a bi-stage diffusion framework.  First, a Diffusion-Controlled Semantic Learner (DSL) models the semantic space of visual features. Second, a Diffusion-Controlled Semantic Translator (DST) learns the distribution of textual features from the semantic space. A Progressive Feature Interaction Network (PFIN) integrates textual information into mapped features step-by-step. The authors demonstrate superior performance over existing methods on multiple datasets (NUS-WIDE, VIREO Food-172, MSRVTT).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its use of diffusion models for cross-modal alignment and the introduction of a semantic space as an intermediary. While diffusion models have been applied to other discriminative tasks, their application to cross-modal alignment, specifically in the LUPI (Learning Using Privileged Information) setting, is a contribution. The bi-stage diffusion process (DSL followed by DST) and the progressive feature interaction are also novel components.

*   **Significance:** Cross-modal alignment is a well-established area, but the challenges of modality heterogeneity and semantic ambiguity remain. The paper's approach of explicitly modeling a semantic space and using a diffusion process to progressively align features addresses these challenges. The empirical results demonstrating improvements over state-of-the-art methods on benchmark datasets support the significance of the contribution.  The claim of model-agnosticism (applicable to different visual backbones) is also a potentially valuable aspect.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Clear Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the limitations of existing cross-modal alignment methods.
    *   **Well-Defined Approach:**  The SeDA method is well-explained, with detailed descriptions of the DSL, DST, and PFIN modules. The bi-stage process is logical and well-motivated.
    *   **Strong Empirical Results:**  The experimental evaluation is comprehensive, including comparisons with multiple baselines and ablation studies to assess the contribution of each module.
    *   **Model Agnostic**: The results demonstrate the effectiveness of SeDA across different backbones.
    *   **Robustness Analysis**: Experiments regarding the hyperparameter selection strategy improve the reliability of the paper.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Complexity:** The proposed method is relatively complex compared to simpler alignment techniques. The increased computational cost might be a limiting factor for some applications. The trade-off between performance gains and computational overhead needs to be considered.
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity:** Although the paper analyzes the robustness of SeDA on hyperparameters, diffusion models, in general, can be sensitive to hyperparameter tuning. Providing more guidance on how to set the hyperparameters for new datasets would be beneficial.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification:** While the paper motivates the approach intuitively, a more rigorous theoretical analysis of why the semantic space and diffusion process lead to improved alignment would strengthen the contribution. For example, showing how SeDA mitigates specific statistical differences between the modalities could be added.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in cross-modal alignment, particularly in the LUPI setting. The use of diffusion models for this task is a promising direction, and the concept of modeling a semantic space as an intermediary could be adopted by other researchers. The model-agnostic aspect also increases the potential for widespread adoption.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and technically sound approach to a relevant problem. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating a clear improvement over existing methods. While the complexity of the approach and some remaining questions regarding hyperparameter tuning and theoretical justification prevent it from being a truly groundbreaking work, the contribution is significant. It is well-written and thoroughly evaluated.

### Towards Secure Semantic Transmission In the Era of GenAI: A Diffusion-based Framework
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05724v1)
- **Authors**: Boxiang He, Zihan Chen, Junshan Luo, Chuanhong Liu, Shilian Wang, Fanggang Wang, Tony Q. S. Quek
- **Abstract**: Semantic communication, due to its focus on the transmitting meaning rather than the raw bit data, poses unique security challenges compared to the traditional communication systems. In particular, semantic communication systems are vulnerable to the malicious attacks that focus on the semantic layer, with the intention of understanding or distorting the intended meaning of the transmitted privacy data. Diffusion models, a class of generative artificial intelligence (GenAI), are well-suited for ensuring data security to attack. Through iteratively adding and then removing noise, diffusion models can generate meaningful information despite the presence of the unknown noise. This article proposes a diffusion-based framework to enhance the security of semantic transmission for the attacks including eavesdropping and jamming. Specifically, the proposed framework incorporates both the artificial noise and natural channel noise into the forward process of the diffusion models during the semantic transmission, with the reverse process used to remove noise at the legitimate receiver. In the eavesdropping scenarios, the artificial noise is the friendly noise designed to prevent semantic eavesdropping. In the jamming scenarios, the artificial noise is the malicious jamming generated by the jammer, which disrupts the semantic transmission. The case studies show that the proposed diffusion-based framework is promising in securing the semantic transmission. We also consolidate several broad research directions associated with the proposed framework.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a diffusion-based framework to enhance the security of semantic communication systems against eavesdropping and jamming attacks. It leverages the properties of diffusion models, specifically their robustness to noise and stability, to secure the transmission of meaningful information.  The framework incorporates artificial noise and natural channel noise into the forward diffusion process, while the reverse process removes this noise at the legitimate receiver. The paper presents case studies demonstrating the framework's effectiveness and discusses future research directions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:**  The application of diffusion models to semantic communication security is a relatively novel concept, although not entirely groundbreaking. The strength lies in the tailored approach to address eavesdropping and jamming attacks specifically in this context. Many works address semantic communication and some works address the use of AI and security, so the novelty is in the specific combination and the presented architecture.

*   **Significance:** Securing semantic communication is critical for its wider adoption, especially in sensitive applications.  The framework's potential to enhance robustness and stability in the face of attacks is significant. This is a relatively "hot" topic, so even incremental improvements are valuable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear explanation of the proposed framework.
    *   Well-structured paper with logical flow.
    *   Case studies that demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach in specific attack scenarios.
    *   Discussion of future research directions.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The experimental evaluation appears somewhat limited. More comprehensive benchmarks and comparisons against other security mechanisms for semantic communication would strengthen the validation.  The paper only uses one dataset(Fashion MNIST).
    *   The theoretical analysis could be more rigorous. While the framework is presented, deeper insights into the theoretical underpinnings of its security guarantees would be beneficial.
    *   The "complexity" future direction is somewhat hand-wavy. There's no immediate path proposed in the paper.

*   **Impact:** The paper could influence future research in semantic communication security by providing a practical framework based on diffusion models. However, its impact will depend on further validation and comparison with existing approaches. While interesting, this is a very applied paper. The overall impact can be limited if the work does not provide a general understanding. The work is expected to find its application in some niche fields instead of broad impacts.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel application of diffusion models in a relevant area of semantic communication security. The framework seems promising, and the paper highlights future research directions. However, the limitations in the experimental evaluation and theoretical analysis hold it back from being a truly exceptional contribution. It is more of an early exploration that needs further development and broader validation.

### Automated Learning of Semantic Embedding Representations for Diffusion Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05732v1)
- **Authors**: Limai Jiang, Yunpeng Cai
- **Abstract**: Generative models capture the true distribution of data, yielding semantically rich representations. Denoising diffusion models (DDMs) exhibit superior generative capabilities, though efficient representation learning for them are lacking. In this work, we employ a multi-level denoising autoencoder framework to expand the representation capacity of DDMs, which introduces sequentially consistent Diffusion Transformers and an additional timestep-dependent encoder to acquire embedding representations on the denoising Markov chain through self-conditional diffusion learning. Intuitively, the encoder, conditioned on the entire diffusion process, compresses high-dimensional data into directional vectors in latent under different noise levels, facilitating the learning of image embeddings across all timesteps. To verify the semantic adequacy of embeddings generated through this approach, extensive experiments are conducted on various datasets, demonstrating that optimally learned embeddings by DDMs surpass state-of-the-art self-supervised representation learning methods in most cases, achieving remarkable discriminative semantic representation quality. Our work justifies that DDMs are not only suitable for generative tasks, but also potentially advantageous for general-purpose deep learning applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a framework, DiER (Diffusion Embedding Representations), for learning semantic embedding representations from denoising diffusion models (DDMs).  DiER transforms DDMs into multi-level denoising autoencoders (DAEs) by encoding timesteps and employing self-conditional diffusion learning. This involves using sequential consistent Diffusion Transformers (DiTs) and an additional timestep-dependent encoder to acquire embedding representations along the denoising Markov chain. The encoder compresses high-dimensional data into directional vectors at different noise levels. The authors conduct experiments on various datasets to verify the semantic adequacy of these embeddings, comparing them against state-of-the-art self-supervised representation learning methods.  The results suggest that optimally learned embeddings from DDMs can achieve remarkable discriminative semantic representation quality and have the potential to be advantageous for general-purpose deep learning applications, not just generative tasks.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of transforming DDMs into multi-level DAEs to learn semantic embeddings is interesting and could be considered novel. Prior work has explored DDMs for zero-shot classification or feature distillation, but this paper takes a different angle by explicitly drawing the DAE analogy and encoding timesteps for improved representation learning. However, the architecture relies on existing components such as DiTs, and adaLN which may temper the originality factor.

*   **Significance:** Demonstrating that DDMs can learn discriminative representations is a valuable contribution. Given the increasing prominence of diffusion models, understanding their potential beyond generative tasks opens up new avenues for research and applications. The paper’s empirical results showing superior performance to other self-supervised methods on some datasets are promising, particularly highlighting an efficiency in representation learning in small-space environments.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper provides a clear explanation of the DiER framework and its connection to DAEs.
    *   The experimental setup is thorough, with comparisons against multiple strong baselines and across diverse datasets.
    *   The observation that representation quality varies across timesteps is insightful and highlights a critical aspect for transferring DDMs to other tasks.
    *   The visual analysis using t-SNE provides qualitative support for the quantitative results.
    *   The discussions of the optimal timestep selection related to image content, and relationship of generative capacity to downstream usefulness is helpful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The choice of DiT as a backbone, while justified, might limit the generalizability of the results. Exploring other architectures would strengthen the findings.
    *   While DiER outperforms SOTA on some datasets, it falls short on others (BCCD and MNIST). A deeper analysis of why this occurs and under what conditions DiER excels is needed.
    *   The computational cost of DiT plus the encoder is a potential drawback, and the paper acknowledges this limitation. However, discussion about alternative approaches should be in the paper.
    *   The "black box" nature of diffusion models makes it difficult to fully understand *why* DiER works. The analysis could benefit from more in-depth investigations into the learned representations and how they capture semantic information.
    *   The reliance on linear probing, while standard, only captures one aspect of the learned representation. More sophisticated evaluation techniques could provide a more comprehensive assessment.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence the field by encouraging further research on:

    *   Developing more efficient architectures for learning semantic representations in DDMs.
    *   Investigating the properties of learned embeddings in DDMs and how they relate to generative capabilities.
    *   Exploring the application of DDMs to a wider range of downstream tasks beyond classification.
    *   Developing methods for automatically selecting the optimal timestep for representation learning.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel approach to learning semantic representations from diffusion models with convincing empirical results on select datasets. The weaknesses related to architectural choices, computational cost, the uneven performance across datasets, and limited interpretability prevent it from achieving a higher score. The significance of the paper lies in bridging the gap between DDMs and self-supervised representation learning, while the technical novelty is reasonable.

### Multimodal Integrated Knowledge Transfer to Large Language Models through Preference Optimization with Biomedical Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05736v1)
- **Authors**: Da Wu, Zhanliang Wang, Quan Nguyen, Zhuoran Xu, Kai Wang
- **Abstract**: The scarcity of high-quality multimodal biomedical data limits the ability to effectively fine-tune pretrained Large Language Models (LLMs) for specialized biomedical tasks. To address this challenge, we introduce MINT (Multimodal Integrated kNowledge Transfer), a framework that aligns unimodal large decoder models with domain-specific decision patterns from multimodal biomedical data through preference optimization. While MINT supports different optimization techniques, we primarily implement it with the Odds Ratio Preference Optimization (ORPO) framework as its backbone. This strategy enables the aligned LLMs to perform predictive tasks using text-only or image-only inputs while retaining knowledge learnt from multimodal data. MINT leverages an upstream multimodal machine learning (MML) model trained on high-quality multimodal data to transfer domain-specific insights to downstream text-only or image-only LLMs. We demonstrate its effectiveness through two key applications: (1) Rare genetic disease prediction from texts, where MINT uses a multimodal encoder model, trained on facial photos and clinical notes, to generate a preference dataset for aligning a lightweight Llama 3.2-3B-Instruct. Despite relying on text input only, the MINT-derived model outperforms models trained with SFT, RAG, or DPO, and even outperforms Llama 3.1-405B-Instruct. (2) Tissue type classification using cell nucleus images, where MINT uses a vision-language foundation model as the preference generator, containing knowledge learnt from both text and histopathological images to align downstream image-only models. The resulting MINT-derived model significantly improves the performance of Llama 3.2-Vision-11B-Instruct on tissue type classification. In summary, MINT provides an effective strategy to align unimodal LLMs with high-quality multimodal expertise through preference optimization.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Multimodal Integrated Knowledge Transfer (MINT), a framework for fine-tuning Large Language Models (LLMs) to perform specialized biomedical tasks using preference optimization. MINT leverages high-quality multimodal biomedical data to transfer domain-specific insights to downstream text-only or image-only LLMs. The framework uses an upstream multimodal machine learning (MML) model to generate a preference learning dataset (preferred vs. unfavored responses) that then guides LLM alignment using Odds Ratio Preference Optimization (ORPO). The authors demonstrate MINT's effectiveness in two applications: rare genetic disease prediction from clinical texts and tissue type classification from cell nucleus images. MINT demonstrates strong performance, especially in rare disease prediction, outperforming methods such as supervised fine-tuning (SFT) and retrieval-augmented generation (RAG).

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the MINT framework's approach to knowledge transfer via preference optimization from multimodal data to unimodal LLMs. It provides a structured way to infuse domain-specific insights without extensive direct fine-tuning. The use of ORPO to directly optimize for preferred outputs in LLMs is a significant methodological contribution in the biomedical space. The approach of grafting the strength of encoder models in classification tasks into large decoder models to enhance reasoning, improve predictive tasks and reduce hallucination in biomedical applications is also very interesting. The framework is also evaluated over many LLM architectures.

*   **Significance:** MINT addresses a critical challenge in applying LLMs to biomedical domains: the scarcity of high-quality labeled multimodal data and the potential for fine-tuning to degrade general reasoning abilities. The reported performance gains in both rare disease prediction and tissue type classification highlight MINT's practical utility. It offers a viable alternative to traditional fine-tuning and retrieval augmented generation (RAG). The consistent increase of performance with ORPO and various models also increases its significance.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Well-defined framework with clear steps.
    *   Rigorous experimental evaluation on two relevant biomedical tasks.
    *   Comparison against strong baselines.
    *   Demonstration of generalization ability across different LLM architectures and sizes.
    *   Address the weakness of the upstream model by combining MINT with retrieval mechanisms

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The performance of MINT diminishes significantly in zero-shot settings when encountering entirely novel disease classes. The lack of diversity on the data is a problem, which the authors recognized.
    *   The quality of MINT’s performance is dependent on the strength of the upstream models used.
    *   MINT still lags specialized encoder-based models in classification, indicating a performance ceiling imposed by the generative architecture of LLMs.
    *   The authors show that MINT-derived model outperforms models trained with direct preference optimization (DPO), but don't provide the experiments' settings for DPO. It is not easy to compare if the method performs better.

*   **Potential Influence:** MINT has the potential to influence the development of more effective LLM-based solutions in biomedicine. Its preference optimization approach could be adapted to other domains with limited labeled data. The framework may also encourage further research into hybrid models that combine the strengths of encoder and decoder architectures, like combining RAG + MINT, that may enhance future research in the biomedicine domain.

**Score:** 7.5

**Rationale:** The paper makes a solid contribution with its MINT framework, demonstrating a novel approach to transferring multimodal knowledge to LLMs through preference optimization and delivering significant performance gains in challenging biomedical tasks. The extensive experiments over different models makes it a solid contribution. However, the limitations in zero-shot generalization and dependence on upstream model quality hold it back from a higher score. Also, the lack of experimentation details on DPO limits the interpretation of its benefits. Nevertheless, the paper presents a valuable approach with demonstrable benefits and a clear potential for future research and practical application.

- **Classification**: q-bio.QM
- **Score**: 7/10

### Harnessing LLMs Explanations to Boost Surrogate Models in Tabular Data Classification
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05744v1)
- **Authors**: Ruxue Shi, Hengrui Gu, Xu Shen, Xin Wang
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have shown remarkable ability in solving complex tasks, making them a promising tool for enhancing tabular learning. However, existing LLM-based methods suffer from high resource requirements, suboptimal demonstration selection, and limited interpretability, which largely hinder their prediction performance and application in the real world. To overcome these problems, we propose a novel in-context learning framework for tabular prediction. The core idea is to leverage the explanations generated by LLMs to guide a smaller, locally deployable Surrogate Language Model (SLM) to make interpretable tabular predictions. Specifically, our framework mainly involves three stages: (i) Post Hoc Explanation Generation, where LLMs are utilized to generate explanations for question-answer pairs in candidate demonstrations, providing insights into the reasoning behind the answer. (ii) Post Hoc Explanation-Guided Demonstrations Selection, which utilizes explanations generated by LLMs to guide the process of demonstration selection from candidate demonstrations. (iii) Post Hoc Explanation-Guided Interpretable SLM Prediction, which utilizes the demonstrations obtained in step (ii) as in-context and merges corresponding explanations as rationales to improve the performance of SLM and guide the model to generate interpretable outputs. Experimental results highlight the framework's effectiveness, with an average accuracy improvement of 5.31% across various tabular datasets in diverse domains.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel in-context learning framework for tabular data classification that leverages the explanations generated by Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve the performance and interpretability of Surrogate Language Models (SLMs).  The core idea is to use LLMs to generate explanations for potential demonstration examples, select demonstrations based on these explanations, and then use the selected demonstrations and explanations to guide a smaller, locally deployable SLM to make interpretable predictions. The framework consists of three stages: (i) Post Hoc Explanation Generation, (ii) Post Hoc Explanation-Guided Demonstrations Selection, and (iii) Post Hoc Explanation-Guided Interpretable SLM Prediction.  Experimental results on several tabular datasets show improved accuracy and interpretability compared to existing methods.  The method seeks to overcome challenges like high resource requirements, suboptimal demonstration selection, and limited interpretability of previous LLM-based tabular learning approaches.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its effective combination of several techniques.  Using LLMs for *post hoc* explanation generation to guide demonstration selection for smaller SLMs is a relatively innovative approach to address the resource constraints and interpretability issues of end-to-end LLM tabular learning.  The idea of filtering tabular features according to feature importance derived from LLM-generated explanations, thereby reducing the impact of noise, adds another layer of novelty.  Existing approaches often focused on using LLMs directly or primarily relied on embedding similarity for demonstration selection.

*   **Significance:** The potential significance is moderate.  Tabular data learning is a crucial area, and the paper addresses some key limitations of applying LLMs to this domain.  The improved accuracy and interpretability demonstrated in the experiments are valuable. Furthermore, the proposed framework reduces the dependency on costly API calls to large LLMs, making the approach more practical for deployment.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Improved Performance:**  The experiments demonstrate consistent improvements in classification accuracy compared to several baselines.
    *   **Enhanced Interpretability:** The framework generates interpretable predictions by incorporating explanations derived from LLMs as rationales, offering insights into the model's decision-making process.
    *   **Resource Efficiency:**  The proposed framework reduces dependency on resource-intensive LLMs by using a smaller SLM guided by LLM-generated explanations, which are generated offline.
    *   **Ablation Studies:** The ablation studies are effective in highlighting the importance of each component of the framework.
    *   **Hyperparameter Analysis:** The parameter sensitivity analyses, although relatively brief, are useful.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on LLM Quality:** The framework's performance is still inherently tied to the quality of the explanations generated by the initial LLM. While the paper addresses this to some extent, it doesn't completely eliminate the LLM dependency. If the initial LLM struggles to generate meaningful explanations, the entire framework suffers.
    *   **Limited Scope of Interpretability Evaluation:** While the framework provides explanations, the paper lacks a comprehensive evaluation of the quality or usefulness of these explanations from a human perspective.  Are the explanations actually insightful and helpful to users? This aspect could be explored further.
    *   **Dataset Diversity:** The datasets used are somewhat limited in scope. While covering several domains, expanding the evaluation to more diverse and complex tabular datasets could further validate the framework's generalizability.
    *   **SLM Architecture Consideration:**  The paper uses Llama2-7B as the SLM. A more thorough exploration and justification for using this specific model compared to others, or even evaluating different SLM architectures, would be valuable. The model hyperparameters are not described.
    *   **Limited comparison to SOTA methods:** The experiments do not compare against some of the latest state-of-the-art methods for tabular data.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper could influence future research by encouraging the use of LLM-generated explanations to guide and improve the performance of smaller, more deployable models for tabular data.  It also highlights the importance of carefully selecting demonstrations in in-context learning and suggests a practical approach to address this challenge.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper presents a well-structured and clearly articulated approach to a significant problem. The experimental results demonstrate the effectiveness of the framework, and the ablation studies provide valuable insights. However, the limitations mentioned above, particularly the lack of a thorough interpretability evaluation and the somewhat limited dataset diversity, prevent it from achieving a higher score. The absence of comparison against very recent SOTA methods also reduces the contribution.

### Insertion Language Models: Sequence Generation with Arbitrary-Position Insertions
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05755v1)
- **Authors**: Dhruvesh Patel, Aishwarya Sahoo, Avinash Amballa, Tahira Naseem, Tim G. J. Rudner, Andrew McCallum
- **Abstract**: Autoregressive models (ARMs), which predict subsequent tokens one-by-one ``from left to right,'' have achieved significant success across a wide range of sequence generation tasks. However, they struggle to accurately represent sequences that require satisfying sophisticated constraints or whose sequential dependencies are better addressed by out-of-order generation. Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) address some of these limitations, but the process of unmasking multiple tokens simultaneously in MDMs can introduce incoherences, and MDMs cannot handle arbitrary infilling constraints when the number of tokens to be filled in is not known in advance. In this work, we introduce Insertion Language Models (ILMs), which learn to insert tokens at arbitrary positions in a sequence -- that is, they select jointly both the position and the vocabulary element to be inserted. By inserting tokens one at a time, ILMs can represent strong dependencies between tokens, and their ability to generate sequences in arbitrary order allows them to accurately model sequences where token dependencies do not follow a left-to-right sequential structure. To train ILMs, we propose a tailored network parameterization and use a simple denoising objective. Our empirical evaluation demonstrates that ILMs outperform both ARMs and MDMs on common planning tasks. Furthermore, we show that ILMs outperform MDMs and perform on par with ARMs in an unconditional text generation task while offering greater flexibility than MDMs in arbitrary-length text infilling.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Insertion Language Models (ILMs), a new sequence generation approach.  Unlike Autoregressive Models (ARMs) that generate tokens sequentially from left to right and Masked Diffusion Models (MDMs) that generate in arbitrary order by iteratively unmasking tokens in parallel, ILMs learn to insert tokens at arbitrary positions in a sequence, one at a time. This involves jointly selecting both the position and vocabulary element to insert. The authors argue this approach allows ILMs to represent strong token dependencies and accurately model sequences where dependencies don't follow a left-to-right structure. They propose a tailored network parameterization, a simplified denoising objective, and empirically demonstrate that ILMs outperform ARMs and MDMs on common planning tasks. They also claim ILMs perform comparably to ARMs in unconditional text generation and outperform MDMs in the same setting and arbitrary-length text infilling, while offering more flexibility.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of generating sequences via arbitrary-position insertions is novel. The method deviates from both the autoregressive and masked diffusion approaches. It carves out a middle ground: tokens are generated one at a time, offering better coherence than MDMs, but not constrained to a left-to-right order like ARMs.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Strengths:** The strength lies in its potential to handle complex sequence generation tasks that require sophisticated constraints or have non-sequential dependencies. The experiments involving star graph generation and Zebra puzzles illustrate this point well. The ability to perform arbitrary-length text infilling is also a significant advantage over MDMs. The demonstration that the approach can learn on standard text datasets and be competitive (or perform on par) with ARMs, suggests it has broader applicability.
    *   **Weaknesses:** A primary weakness is that the benefits compared to ARMs on *general* text generation are not overwhelming. While the method is competitive, there's no clear demonstration of a significant improvement over ARMs *on standard language modeling tasks*. The paper notes that the ILMs are slower than ARMs and have difficulty learning on large datasets, which shows the potential for improvement that can improve the usefulness of the method.

*   **Rigor:** The empirical evaluation includes synthetic planning tasks and text generation, offering a reasonable range of evaluations. However, the lack of significant improvement on standard text generation tasks and the inability of the model to be trained on the larger datasets is not a strong point in its favor. The paper would be further strengthed by a more detailed analysis of the complexities of the methods in comparison to ARMs and MDMs and scaling improvements that might occur for larger models or dataset sizes.

*   **Potential Impact:** If ILMs can be further developed to improve performance on general language modeling tasks and scaled to larger models and datasets, they could become a valuable tool for tasks requiring precise control over the generated sequence, constraint satisfaction, or flexible infilling. The approach might also inspire new architectures that combine the strengths of ARMs, MDMs, and ILMs.

**Justification for the Score:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to sequence generation with promising characteristics.  The results on planning tasks and arbitrary-length infilling are notable. However, the incremental improvements in general text generation compared to ARMs, and the noted limitation in scaling and efficiency are downsides. I believe the paper has the potential to be a good contribution with improvement in its shortcomings.

### Evolutionary thoughts: integration of large language models and evolutionary algorithms
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05756v1)
- **Authors**: Antonio Jimeno Yepes, Pieter Barnard
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) have unveiled remarkable capabilities in understanding and generating both natural language and code, but LLM reasoning is prone to hallucination and struggle with complex, novel scenarios, often getting stuck on partial or incorrect solutions. However, the inherent ability of Evolutionary Algorithms (EAs) to explore extensive and complex search spaces makes them particularly effective in scenarios where traditional optimization methodologies may falter. However, EAs explore a vast search space when applied to complex problems. To address the computational bottleneck of evaluating large populations, particularly crucial for complex evolutionary tasks, we introduce a highly efficient evaluation framework. This implementation maintains compatibility with existing primitive definitions, ensuring the generation of valid individuals. Using LLMs, we propose an enhanced evolutionary search strategy that enables a more focused exploration of expansive solution spaces. LLMs facilitate the generation of superior candidate solutions, as evidenced by empirical results demonstrating their efficacy in producing improved outcomes.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an integration of Large Language Models (LLMs) and Evolutionary Algorithms (EAs) to enhance program synthesis. The core idea is to use LLMs to guide the evolutionary search process, specifically during the initial population generation and mutation steps, to overcome the limitations of random exploration in traditional EAs. It further addresses the computational bottleneck of evaluating large populations by introducing a fast, C++-based evaluation framework, optimized for both CPU and CUDA GPU environments. The effectiveness of this hybrid approach is evaluated on a set of program synthesis tasks, including list manipulation tasks like counting, finding min/max, inverting, and sorting.  The results demonstrate improved performance, particularly in terms of accuracy and function length, compared to standard EAs. The paper also explores an ensembling approach by combining the top individuals from multiple runs to achieve more robust solutions.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The integration of LLMs with EAs is not entirely novel; however, the specific application to program synthesis with a focus on guiding initial population generation and mutation *and* a highly optimized evaluation framework adds a layer of practical contribution. Prior work has explored LLM-guided search but this paper presents a concrete implementation and quantitative evaluation focusing on the efficiency of the hybrid system. The ensembling approach is a standard technique in evolutionary computing, but the paper demonstrates its value in conjunction with LLM guidance. The novelty lies in the specific combination of LLM guidance, fast evaluation implementation and demonstrating quantitative improvements for symbolic regression problems.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in addressing the inherent limitations of EAs when dealing with complex program synthesis tasks. EAs often struggle with large search spaces and premature convergence, and the LLM integration offers a way to inject semantic understanding and targeted guidance, potentially making EAs more effective in these scenarios. The fast evaluation framework tackles the computational cost associated with large populations, which is crucial for scaling EAs to more complex problems. The demonstrated improvements on program synthesis tasks, even if limited to list manipulation, suggest a promising direction for future research and applications. However, the scope of tested problems is limited, and the conclusions drawn from these results need more broader empirical validation.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Clear problem statement and well-defined approach.
    *   Concrete implementation and quantitative evaluation.
    *   Performance improvements compared to standard EAs.
    *   Fast evaluation framework addresses a critical bottleneck.
    *   Addresses the diversity trade-off within EAs that LLMs can negatively affect.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limited scope of evaluation tasks (list manipulation only).
    *   Reliance on synthetic datasets. Real-world programming problems often have different characteristics and degrees of complexity.
    *   The specific LLM prompting strategies and choices aren't discussed in sufficient detail, and no ablation study is done to explore prompting choices.
    *   The paper states, "We did not use an LLM to perform crossover, which could be explored as future work." This is a clear area for potential exploration and future research.
    *   The paper focuses on smaller problems, which limits the demonstration of its scalability.
    *   The claim about warp divergence within CUDA leading to slower CPU implementation is suspect and needs more evaluation. Often optimized CPU code with multi-threading can outperform naive GPU kernels.

*   **Potential Influence:**
    *   The paper can inspire further research on integrating LLMs with EAs for program synthesis and other complex optimization tasks.
    *   The fast evaluation framework can be a valuable resource for researchers working with EAs.
    *   The ensembling approach highlights the importance of robustness in LLM-guided EAs.

**Justification:**

While the paper demonstrates a promising integration of LLMs and EAs for program synthesis, its limited scope of evaluation and other weaknesses prevents it from being a groundbreaking contribution. The paper needs to address a more diverse suite of problems. The reliance on synthetic data also raises questions about its generalizability to more real-world tasks. However, the paper's clear problem statement, concrete implementation, and demonstrated performance improvements justify a reasonable score.

### APOLLO: Automated LLM and Lean Collaboration for Advanced Formal Reasoning
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05758v1)
- **Authors**: Azim Ospanov, Roozbeh Yousefzadeh
- **Abstract**: Formal reasoning and automated theorem proving constitute a challenging subfield of machine learning, in which machines are tasked with proving mathematical theorems using formal languages like Lean. A formal verification system can check whether a formal proof is correct or not almost instantaneously, but generating a completely correct formal proof with large language models (LLMs) remains a formidable task. The usual approach in the literature is to prompt the LLM many times (up to several thousands) until one of the generated proofs passes the verification system. In this work, we present APOLLO (Automated PrOof repair via LLM and Lean cOllaboration), a modular, model-agnostic pipeline that combines the strengths of the Lean compiler with an LLM's reasoning abilities to achieve better proof-generation results at a low sampling budget. Apollo directs a fully automated process in which the LLM generates proofs for theorems, a set of agents analyze the proofs, fix the syntax errors, identify the mistakes in the proofs using Lean, isolate failing sub-lemmas, utilize automated solvers, and invoke an LLM on each remaining goal with a low top-K budget. The repaired sub-proofs are recombined and reverified, iterating up to a user-controlled maximum number of attempts. On the miniF2F benchmark, we establish a new state-of-the-art accuracy of 75.0% among 7B-parameter models while keeping the sampling budget below one thousand. Moreover, Apollo raises the state-of-the-art accuracy for Goedel-Prover-SFT to 65.6% while cutting sample complexity from 25,600 to a few hundred. General-purpose models (o3-mini, o4-mini) jump from 3-7% to over 40% accuracy. Our results demonstrate that targeted, compiler-guided repair of LLM outputs yields dramatic gains in both efficiency and correctness, suggesting a general paradigm for scalable automated theorem proving.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper, aiming for rigor and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper introduces APOLLO, a novel and fully automated pipeline for automated theorem proving (ATP) in the Lean formal verification system.  APOLLO aims to improve the efficiency and accuracy of Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating formally correct proofs.  It achieves this by combining the reasoning abilities of LLMs with the strict error detection capabilities of the Lean compiler.  The pipeline involves a modular agent-based approach: 1) Syntax Refiner fixes syntax errors in LLM outputs, 2) a "Sorrifier" inserts `sorry` placeholders to mark incomplete proof steps, 3) an Auto Solver attempts to solve the remaining goals automatically, and 4) recursive application of the LLM on each remaining `sorry` goal with a low top-K budget, and finally 5) Proof Assembler combines sub-proofs back into a complete proof. The framework is model-agnostic and allows for collaboration between LLMs, the Lean compiler, and automated solvers.  The paper demonstrates that APOLLO significantly improves the state-of-the-art accuracy on the miniF2F benchmark, especially for smaller LLMs (e.g. 7B parameter models), while simultaneously reducing the sample complexity (the number of LLM calls) compared to simply generating whole proofs repeatedly.  The authors highlight the benefits of compiler-guided repair in achieving these results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The central idea of using the Lean compiler as a guide to repair and refine LLM-generated proofs is a significant innovation.  While prior work exists on using feedback to repair proofs, Apollo innovatively integrates this feedback into a modular, agent-based pipeline, allowing for targeted LLM calls on specific sub-problems. The modular design and specific techniques within each module (e.g., how the "Sorrifier" handles compilation errors) contributes to the novelty. This approach is a departure from the standard "whole-proof generation" paradigm prevalent in the field. Furthermore, the system's reliance on a low top-K budget for LLM calls contrasts with the previous standard practice of high-sampling for proof attempts.

* **Significance:**  The demonstrated results on the miniF2F benchmark are compelling.  Achieving state-of-the-art accuracy with a significantly reduced sampling budget is a valuable contribution.  The fact that APOLLO unlocks performance in smaller, general-purpose models (e.g., OpenAI's 03-mini/04-mini) is also noteworthy, suggesting a path towards more efficient and accessible ATP. The paper's impact lies in offering a scalable approach to automated theorem proving by utilizing existing tools and formalisms (compiler and automated solvers). A system that makes ATP more efficient may also lead to more research and exploration in this field.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Clear and well-defined pipeline:** The modular architecture of APOLLO is well-explained and makes it easy to understand and potentially extend.
    * **Strong empirical results:** The performance improvements on miniF2F are significant and backed by thorough experimentation and comparisons to existing methods.  The analysis of proof lengths and the impact of the recursion depth parameter provide valuable insights.
    * **Model-agnostic design:** APOLLO is compatible with a variety of LLMs, increasing its generalizability and potential impact.
    * **Emphasis on efficiency:**  The reduction in sample complexity is a crucial consideration for the practical application of ATP.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on base model quality:** The paper acknowledges that APOLLO's effectiveness is dependent on the quality of the initial proof sketch generated by the LLM.  While the system can repair many errors, it may struggle with fundamentally flawed proof strategies. This dependence limits the applicability of APOLLO to base models with sufficient reasoning capabilities. Further investigation into the minimum quality of input needed may have been useful.
    * **Limited exploration of tree-search integration:**  The limitations section mentions that APOLLO is not integrated with tree-search methods. Expanding into these would potentially add value to their tool.
    * **miniF2F focus:**  The evaluation is largely confined to the miniF2F benchmark.  While this is a standard dataset, it would be beneficial to see APOLLO's performance on other ATP benchmarks or real-world mathematical problems.
    * **"Sampling budget" discussion is convoluted.** It could have been clearer that there were two distinct metrics that were being conflated -- one as the sampling budget for a wholistic approach, and the other as the average sampling per "proof" from Apollo

* **Potential Influence:** APOLLO has the potential to significantly influence the field of ATP. The compiler-guided repair paradigm may become a standard approach, and the modular design could inspire future research on more sophisticated agent-based ATP systems. Its emphasis on efficiency makes it more accessible. The system may promote further collaboration between AI researchers and mathematicians by providing a platform for leveraging formal verification tools.

**Justification of Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field of ATP by introducing APOLLO. The system's architecture, empirical results, and potential impact are noteworthy. While it has some limitations (especially dependence on base-model quality), the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. APOLLO advances the field by demonstrating a more efficient and accurate approach to combining LLMs with formal verification tools. Therefore, it merits a high score.

### Multi-Agent Systems for Robotic Autonomy with LLMs
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05762v1)
- **Authors**: Junhong Chen, Ziqi Yang, Haoyuan G Xu, Dandan Zhang, George Mylonas
- **Abstract**: Since the advent of Large Language Models (LLMs), various research based on such models have maintained significant academic attention and impact, especially in AI and robotics. In this paper, we propose a multi-agent framework with LLMs to construct an integrated system for robotic task analysis, mechanical design, and path generation. The framework includes three core agents: Task Analyst, Robot Designer, and Reinforcement Learning Designer. Outputs are formatted as multimodal results, such as code files or technical reports, for stronger understandability and usability. To evaluate generalizability comparatively, we conducted experiments with models from both GPT and DeepSeek. Results demonstrate that the proposed system can design feasible robots with control strategies when appropriate task inputs are provided, exhibiting substantial potential for enhancing the efficiency and accessibility of robotic system development in research and industrial applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes a multi-agent framework leveraging Large Language Models (LLMs) for robotic autonomy. The framework encompasses three core agents: Task Analyst, Robot Designer, and Reinforcement Learning Designer.  These agents collaboratively analyze task requirements, design robot configurations, and generate reinforcement learning (RL) strategies for task execution. The framework aims to streamline robotic system development by providing multimodal outputs like code and technical reports for enhanced usability. The authors test the framework using LLMs from OpenAI (GPT) and DeepSeek on various tasks. The results suggest the system can design robots with feasible control strategies, potentially improving the efficiency and accessibility of robotic system development. Experiments were conducted to assess generalizability across tasks and the impact of individual agents through ablation studies.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents a potentially useful framework for automating aspects of robotic system design, leveraging the strengths of LLMs. The modular design of the multi-agent system is a good approach, enabling flexibility and scalability. The inclusion of code extraction and report generation is also a pragmatic addition, addressing the need for actionable outputs.  The experiments evaluating generalizability and the contribution of each agent provide some insights into the system's performance.

However, several aspects require critical examination:

*   **Novelty:** While the application of LLMs in robotics is an active area of research, the specific combination of these three agents (Task Analyst, Robot Designer, RL Designer) and the specific workflow described may offer incremental novelty. The real novelty lies in the implementation details and the effectiveness of the prompts used to guide the LLMs, which are not thoroughly explained. Similar architectures have been implemented in other works and therefore its novelty is questionable
*   **Significance:** The significance hinges on whether this framework demonstrably outperforms existing approaches or provides a significantly more efficient workflow. The paper claims enhanced efficiency but lacks quantitative comparisons against traditional robotic development methods. Without such benchmarks, it's difficult to assess the practical impact. Furthermore, the reliance on LLMs introduces potential issues of bias, hallucination, and a lack of explainability, which are not adequately addressed.
*   **Evaluation:** The evaluation metrics (TCP, CEF, MA, RDA, RM) are a mix of objective and subjective measures.  The subjective measures (RDA, RM) are particularly vulnerable to bias, even with multiple evaluators.  A more rigorous quantitative evaluation, including metrics like task completion time, success rate, and robot energy consumption, would strengthen the results. The paper mentions OpenAI Gym is used, but the exact experimental setup is not well defined and thus the repeatability cannot be guaranteed. The generalizability is tested across ten tasks, divided into industrial and medical, but a broader range of tasks and environments is needed to fully assess the framework's robustness.
*   **Implementation details:** The paper is short on details regarding the prompts, code, and hyperparameters used. A proper description of the prompt engineering used in this work can add significant value to this work.
*   **Clarity:** While the overall concept is understandable, the descriptions of the specific interactions between agents and the decision-making processes of the LLMs could be more detailed. Some of the jargon could be described in more details for the average audience in robotics.

**Strengths:**

*   Modular framework design.
*   Focus on generating actionable outputs (code, reports).
*   Evaluation of generalizability and agent contributions (though limited).

**Weaknesses:**

*   Limited novelty in the overall architecture.
*   Lack of quantitative performance comparisons.
*   Potential for bias and lack of explainability due to reliance on LLMs.
*   Reliance on subjective metrics.
*   Inadequate description of implementation details (prompts, code).
*   Limited range of tested tasks and environments.

**Score:** 5

**Rationale:**

The paper presents an interesting idea, and contributes incrementally to the use of LLMs in robotic autonomy. However, the limited novelty, lack of robust quantitative evaluation, potential for bias, and inadequate implementation details prevent it from being a highly significant contribution. There is a lack of in-depth novelty that it deserves higher score. More details regarding the prompts and robot task setups should be provided, and therefore I deduct some points here.

### Sparse Attention Remapping with Clustering for Efficient LLM Decoding on PIM
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05772v1)
- **Authors**: Zehao Fan, Garrett Gagnon, Zhenyu Liu, Liu Liu
- **Abstract**: Transformer-based models are the foundation of modern machine learning, but their execution, particularly during autoregressive decoding in large language models (LLMs), places significant pressure on memory systems due to frequent memory accesses and growing key-value (KV) caches. This creates a bottleneck in memory bandwidth, especially as context lengths increase. Processing-in-memory (PIM) architectures are a promising solution, offering high internal bandwidth and compute parallelism near memory. However, current PIM designs are primarily optimized for dense attention and struggle with the dynamic, irregular access patterns introduced by modern KV cache sparsity techniques. Consequently, they suffer from workload imbalance, reducing throughput and resource utilization. In this work, we propose STARC, a novel sparsity-optimized data mapping scheme tailored specifically for efficient LLM decoding on PIM architectures. STARC clusters KV pairs by semantic similarity and maps them to contiguous memory regions aligned with PIM bank structures. During decoding, queries retrieve relevant tokens at cluster granularity by matching against precomputed centroids, enabling selective attention and parallel processing without frequent reclustering or data movement overhead. Experiments on the HBM-PIM system show that, compared to common token-wise sparsity methods, STARC reduces attention-layer latency by 19%--31% and energy consumption by 19%--27%. Under a KV cache budget of 1024, it achieves up to 54%--74% latency reduction and 45%--67% energy reduction compared to full KV cache retrieval. Meanwhile, STARC maintains model accuracy comparable to state-of-the-art sparse attention methods, demonstrating its effectiveness in enabling efficient and hardware-friendly long-context LLM inference on PIM architectures.
- **Summary**: Okay, I'll provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the paper, including a novelty/significance score and justification.

**Summary:**

The paper "Sparse Attention Remapping with Clustering for Efficient LLM Decoding on PIM" addresses the challenges of running sparse attention mechanisms in Large Language Models (LLMs) on Processing-in-Memory (PIM) architectures. It observes that existing PIM designs are optimized for dense computations and struggle with the dynamic, irregular access patterns introduced by KV-cache sparsity techniques, leading to workload imbalance and reduced throughput. To address this, the paper proposes STARC, a novel data mapping scheme that clusters semantically similar tokens and maps them to contiguous memory regions aligned with PIM bank structures. During decoding, queries retrieve relevant tokens at cluster granularity by comparing against precomputed centroids, enabling selective attention and parallel processing without frequent reclustering or data movement overhead. Experimental results on an HBM-PIM system demonstrate that STARC reduces attention layer latency and energy consumption compared to token-wise sparsity methods and full KV cache retrieval while maintaining model accuracy.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of clustering semantically similar tokens and remapping them to contiguous memory locations to better align with the row-granularity of PIM architectures is relatively novel in the context of sparse attention in LLMs and PIM. While clustering and remapping are not entirely new concepts, their specific application and integration within a PIM architecture for LLM decoding in conjunction with sparse attention appears to be a unique contribution. Previous works have touched upon memory optimization but typically focus on optimizing dense computation or hardware-friendly strategies like page-wise sparsity without the semantic awareness offered by STARC.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a critical performance bottleneck in LLM inference, namely, the memory access overhead of attention mechanisms. With the growing trend of long-context LLMs, efficient memory access becomes crucial for achieving low latency and high throughput. By adapting the data layout to be PIM-friendly while retaining the advantages of sparsity, STARC has the potential to improve the efficiency of LLM inference on specialized hardware. Furthermore, the performance benefits reported are non-trivial, demonstrating a significant reduction in latency and energy consumption. The maintenance of model accuracy while enhancing speed and energy efficiency further strengthens the significance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   The problem is well-motivated and clearly defined.
    *   The proposed STARC approach is innovative and specifically tailored to the characteristics of PIM architectures.
    *   The experimental evaluation is comprehensive and includes comparisons with relevant baselines, covering both accuracy and performance metrics.
    *   The paper provides a thorough analysis of the results and offers valuable insights into the effectiveness of STARC.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   The evaluation is based on a simulator. While the simulator is AttAcc, it remains a simulation and the result on real hardware may differ.
    *   While it is compared with several sparse attention methods, it doesn't provide a complexity analysis of the clustering algorithm, especially in the online setting. The frequency of re-clustering (every 128 steps) is empirically determined, but the cost of clustering can be a bottleneck.
    *   There is room for a more thorough discussion of the hardware overhead (area, power) associated with implementing the clustering and remapping logic within the PIM architecture.
    *   The paper uses LLaMA-7B for evaluation. Larger models may exhibit different behavior, especially with respect to sparsity patterns.
*   **Impact and Influence:**
    *   The paper can influence future research directions in LLM acceleration on PIM architectures by highlighting the importance of sparsity-aware data mapping schemes.
    *   It may inspire the development of other hardware-software co-design techniques that optimize memory access patterns for specialized hardware.
    *   The proposed STARC approach could be adopted and further improved by other researchers and practitioners in the field.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and practical solution to an important problem in the field of LLM acceleration. While it has some limitations, the strengths outweigh the weaknesses. STARC's demonstrated improvements in performance and energy efficiency, combined with its hardware-friendly design, make it a valuable contribution to the field. I believe this is a good paper with decent novelty and significant impact.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### A Day in Their Shoes: Using LLM-Based Perspective-Taking Interactive Fiction to Reduce Stigma Toward Dirty Work
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05786v1)
- **Authors**: Xiangzhe Yuan, Jiajun Wang, Qian Wan, Siying Hu
- **Abstract**: Occupations referred to as "dirty work" often face entrenched social stigma, which adversely affects the mental health of workers in these fields and impedes occupational equity. In this study, we propose a novel Interactive Fiction (IF) framework powered by Large Language Models (LLMs) to encourage perspective-taking and reduce biases against these stigmatized yet essential roles. Through an experiment with participants (n = 100) across four such occupations, we observed a significant increase in participants' understanding of these occupations, as well as a high level of empathy and a strong sense of connection to individuals in these roles. Additionally, qualitative interviews with participants (n = 15) revealed that the LLM-based perspective-taking IF enhanced immersion, deepened emotional resonance and empathy toward "dirty work," and allowed participants to experience a sense of professional fulfillment in these occupations. However, participants also highlighted ongoing challenges, such as limited contextual details generated by the LLM and the unintentional reinforcement of existing stereotypes. Overall, our findings underscore that an LLM-based perspective-taking IF framework offers a promising and scalable strategy for mitigating stigma and promoting social equity in marginalized professions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to reduce social stigma towards "dirty work" occupations (janitors, firefighters, police officers, and caregivers) using Large Language Model (LLM)-based perspective-taking Interactive Fiction (IF). The authors hypothesized that by immersing users in simulated daily experiences of these workers, they could foster empathy and reduce biases. A study with 100 participants found a significant increase in understanding of these occupations and higher levels of empathy after interacting with the LLM-generated IF. Qualitative interviews further highlighted the enhanced emotional resonance and immersive experience offered by the approach. The paper acknowledges limitations, such as potential reinforcement of stereotypes and limited narrative depth. Overall, it suggests that LLM-based perspective-taking IF is a promising and scalable strategy for mitigating stigma and promoting social equity.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper presents an interesting and timely application of LLMs to a significant social problem: the stigma associated with essential but often devalued occupations. The idea of using interactive fiction, dynamically generated by an LLM, to foster perspective-taking is novel. This allows for personalized and adaptive experiences compared to traditional methods which often rely on predefined scripts.

**Strengths:**

*   **Novelty:** The combination of LLMs, interactive fiction, and perspective-taking for stigma reduction is a unique contribution. Previous research has mostly employed static content, chatbots, or VR, rather than a dynamically generated IF environment.
*   **Relevance:** Addresses a crucial social issue with practical implications for worker well-being and occupational equity.
*   **Empirical Support:** The study employs a mixed-methods approach (quantitative surveys and qualitative interviews) to provide robust evidence for the effectiveness of the intervention.
*   **Clear Research Questions:** The RQs are well-defined and guide the research effectively.
*   **Comprehensive Analysis:**  The paper clearly explains the methodology, results, and limitations of the approach, addressing potential confounding factors (like pre-existing empathy levels) and acknowledging challenges like stereotype reinforcement.
*   **Ethical Considerations:** It mentions getting IRB approval and protecting user privacy.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Generalizability:** The sample is from a single country (China), potentially limiting the generalizability of the findings to other cultural contexts.
*   **Potential for Stereotype Reinforcement:** While acknowledged, the risk of the LLM inadvertently reinforcing existing stereotypes requires careful consideration and mitigation strategies. The paper touches on this but could explore it in more detail.
*   **Reliance on Self-Reported Measures:** The study heavily relies on self-reported empathy and attitudes, which might be subject to social desirability bias. Including behavioral measures could strengthen the findings.
*   **Lack of Comparison to Baseline:** It does not directly compare its new method to the most effective of existing methods. While the comparison might be out of the scope, its efficacy and efficiency are not easily gauged without this.
*   **Modest Effect Sizes:** The quantitative findings indicate statistically significant improvements, but the effect sizes might be moderate, requiring further investigation into the practical significance of the intervention.
*   **Narrative limitations**: Though acknowledged and discussed the authors could have done a better job in exploring the actual challenges of the selected professions.

**Significance:**

The paper is significant because it demonstrates the potential of LLMs beyond simple task automation, showcasing their application in addressing complex social issues. The approach is scalable and potentially more engaging than traditional perspective-taking methods. The findings offer valuable insights for researchers and practitioners interested in using AI for social good. Further, LLMs are being used more and more in HCI research and this particular paper is quite timely.

**Overall:**

While the study has some limitations, it presents a novel and promising approach to reducing stigma. The empirical evidence, clear articulation of limitations, and practical implications justify a moderately high score.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.HC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Demystifying Diffusion Policies: Action Memorization and Simple Lookup Table Alternatives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2505.05787v1)
- **Authors**: Chengyang He, Xu Liu, Gadiel Sznaier Camps, Guillaume Sartoretti, Mac Schwager
- **Abstract**: Diffusion policies have demonstrated remarkable dexterity and robustness in intricate, high-dimensional robot manipulation tasks, while training from a small number of demonstrations. However, the reason for this performance remains a mystery. In this paper, we offer a surprising hypothesis: diffusion policies essentially memorize an action lookup table -- and this is beneficial. We posit that, at runtime, diffusion policies find the closest training image to the test image in a latent space, and recall the associated training action sequence, offering reactivity without the need for action generalization. This is effective in the sparse data regime, where there is not enough data density for the model to learn action generalization. We support this claim with systematic empirical evidence. Even when conditioned on wildly out of distribution (OOD) images of cats and dogs, the Diffusion Policy still outputs an action sequence from the training data. With this insight, we propose a simple policy, the Action Lookup Table (ALT), as a lightweight alternative to the Diffusion Policy. Our ALT policy uses a contrastive image encoder as a hash function to index the closest corresponding training action sequence, explicitly performing the computation that the Diffusion Policy implicitly learns. We show empirically that for relatively small datasets, ALT matches the performance of a diffusion model, while requiring only 0.0034 of the inference time and 0.0085 of the memory footprint, allowing for much faster closed-loop inference with resource constrained robots. We also train our ALT policy to give an explicit OOD flag when the distance between the runtime image is too far in the latent space from the training images, giving a simple but effective runtime monitor. More information can be found at: https://stanfordmsl.github.io/alt/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Demystifying Diffusion Policies: Action Memorization and Simple Lookup Table Alternatives" challenges the prevailing understanding of diffusion policies in robot manipulation. It proposes that diffusion policies, despite their impressive dexterity, largely operate by memorizing training data rather than generalizing action sequences. The authors hypothesize that these policies essentially act as lookup tables, finding the closest training image in latent space and recalling the associated action sequence.  They support this with empirical evidence, including experiments where diffusion policies output actions from the training data even when presented with out-of-distribution (OOD) images.  Based on this insight, they introduce the Action Lookup Table (ALT) policy, a simpler alternative using contrastive image encoding to index training action sequences. The ALT policy achieves comparable performance to diffusion policies on small datasets with significantly reduced inference time and memory footprint.
